#464. Bitdefender Antivirus Plus Review: Undercutting the Competition

Antivirus programs are a dime a dozen these days, and while the vast array of security threats that assail modern devices can at times feel so overwhelming that protection seems impossible, a good antivirus solution remains essential.

Unfortunately, purchasing an antivirus can prove rather confusing. Companies offer a huge range of bundles with long lists of features, many of which are obscured in vague language. If you dig deep enough into a company’s website, however, you’ll find that most still offer the option to purchase the antivirus with few add-ons at a lower price. And if that’s what you want, Bitdefender’s Antivirus Plus is a good option.

Buying Bitdefender Antivirus Plus on its own is arguably a bit confusing, as the company strongly pushes shoppers toward more expensive and all-inclusive Bitdefender bundles, which include other features like a password manager, dark web monitoring, and lots more. In total, there are four different bundles to choose from: Total Security, Premium Security, Ultimate Security, and Ultimate Security Plus Extended, ranging from $60 up to $140 for the first year. There are also Family versions of these plans, which are more expensive but add protection for up to 25 devices and parental controls. All these bundles include the same antivirus protection, but if you search for Antivirus Plus on its own, though, you will find it. And it’s arguably worth seeking out, as Bitdefender's entry-level options are priced most aggressively.

Bitdefender Antivirus Plus currently offers an introductory price of $25 for the first year (and $50 after) for a single device or $30 for the first year ($70 after) for three devices. This is quite a bit less than Bitdefender Total Security, which is $60 for the first year (and $110 after) and only offers two benefits in exchange: a password manager and protection for up to five devices instead of just one. The lower tiers tend to be better deals, especially if you're after something specific.

That’s because Bitdefender’s pricing is most competitive at its lowest tiers, which may by itself be enough reason for many to choose it. Rivals like Norton Antivirus Standard, McAfee Essential, Avira Internet Security, and Malwarebytes Standard typically ask more for a similar level of service.

There is some variation here; Norton throws in a password manager at a lower price point, for instance, and some competitors have superior VPN service included (Bitdefender Antivirus Plus limits you to 200 MB of VPN data per day, which isn’t much). Still, Bitdefender Antivirus Plus is among the more affordable options, particularly if you’re more interested in the antivirus than the add-ons.

Savvy users might balk at paying at all, as many free antivirus options exist. Bitdefender itself offers a free antivirus download for both Windows and Android (but not macOS, iOS, or iPadOS). This is a fair choice, though it often means you’ll have to tolerate advertisements urging you to sign up.

Buying, downloading, and installing Bitdefender Antivirus Plus is a smooth experience. The payment portal is straightforward. The installer is really a downloader, so it’s a small file; the full download required by the installer is roughly 800 megabytes. Once the installation is finished, Antivirus Plus automatically runs an initial virus check. This took less than five minutes on a Microsoft Surface Laptop 7 running Windows 11.

Once installed, the Bitdefender Antivirus Plus interface provides a default dashboard that includes several common actions, including a quick scan, total system scan, and vulnerability scan (the latter looks for problems like a weak account password or an overly permissive network configuration). You’ll also find an option to turn on Bitdefender VPN, which is installed alongside the antivirus—though, as mentioned, you’re limited to 200 MB of data per day unless you upgrade to Bitdefender Premium VPN.

These features are largely standard for an antivirus program, though one extra feature requires more explanation: Safepay. This creates a sandboxed browsing environment meant to thwart threats like keyloggers and man-in-the-middle attacks. To be honest, I’m doubtful of the feature’s utility. I don’t believe these are the most common avenues cybercriminals use to obtain payment information; they’re far more likely to buy huge repositories of payment data stolen from compromised companies. It’s also rather clunky to launch Safepay for an online purchase, so I don’t think most people will want to use it.

I spent most of my time using Bitdefender Antivirus Plus on Windows, which I think is the best version of the software. The Android and iOS versions look largely similar to it, though of course compressed for a mobile device. However, macOS is the odd one out with an interface that looks different from its peers and, to my eyes, seems about five years out of date. It’s not terrible and has similar functionality, but I find this a bit odd. It seems like the macOS software is missing a software refresh that other platforms received.

A nice software interface is a perk but, at its core, antivirus software is supposed to stop viruses and other malware. So, how does Bitdefender Antivirus Plus rank?

Bitdefender scored well in AV-Test’s latest report, where it detected 100 percent of the tested attacks and received an AV-Test Top Product certification. It also received high marks from AV-Comparatives, which found it detected 99.5 percent of threats, enough to earn it the top three-star score. Bitdefender’s consumer products weren’t ranked in the latest SE Labs consumer antivirus report, but the small-business product detected 99 percent of threats and received an AAA ranking in the April 2026 report.

The performance hit from Bitdefender Antivirus Plus wasn’t noticeable in typical use of either my Windows 11 laptop or my Apple MacBook Air M4. Bitdefender services consumed about 700 MB of memory in total on both platforms. That’s not a lot, and it's in line with what I often see from antivirus programs, though it’s also not trivial. I can imagine this could at times result in a performance hit on a device with 8 GB of RAM. My devices have 32 GB and 24 GB, respectively, however, so I didn’t encounter any issues.

Bitdefender Antivirus Plus checks off most boxes for an antivirus program. It’s affordable, easy to use, earns high marks from malware testing labs, and avoids significant performance issues when installed. But I do want to return to a problem I’ve mentioned before: the VPN.

Antivirus Plus advertises an included VPN. This is true, but it’s actually the free VPN service that anyone can obtain. The service has a strict 200-MB (per day, per device) data cap, which isn’t going to be enough if you want to use it as your main VPN. To be fair, Bitdefender does detail this limitation on its website—but it does seem like an added feature when, in reality, it’s available for free whether you buy Antivirus Plus or not.

That could steer you away from Bitdefender if you want an antivirus and VPN. Competitors like Norton and McAfee bundle an unlimited VPN service into lower tiers of service, which, though more expensive than Bitdefender Antivirus Plus, are less expensive than Bitdefender Antivirus Plus and Bitdefender Premium VPN combined.

Unfortunate VPN bundling aside, Bitdefender Antivirus Plus is a good choice if you want antivirus for Windows, macOS, Android, and iOS.

It’s particularly competitive if you just want the antivirus protection on its own. All of the major cybersecurity companies would really prefer it if you bought a bundle (including Bitdefender), and they’re often good value if you actually use all the features. But there are plenty of reasons to just go for the antivirus itself (for example, credit and dark web monitoring features might already be available through your bank or another institution). And if antivirus protection is all you want, Bitdefender Antivirus Plus is a superb choice.

Bitdefender’s advantage is quite simple. It provides antivirus protection that detects most threats at a price that often undercuts the competition. It’s hard to think of a reason to pay more for an alternative when Bitdefender does the job well for as little as $25 for the first year.

#463. Goldman CEO Solomon says AI financing remains strong despite market jitters

Goldman Sachs CEO David Solomon told CNBC on Monday that financing demand for artificial intelligence infrastructure remains strong despite recent market jitters as investors reassess the risks involving the massive build-out.

Solomon said that his firm has been "very active" in AI capital markets deals over recent weeks, a setup that he expects to continue.

"There's still very strong demand for investments in the AI infrastructure build and the technology around the growth of AI," Solomon told CNBC's Leslie Picker.

Changes in the political environment are among factors that will "create recalibrations and ups and downs, and we're seeing some of that," Solomon said. "But I still deeply believe in the long-term trajectory of the power of AI in our economy to drive productivity."

AI-related stocks, including chipmakers and neocloud providers, have tumbled in recent weeks as investors reassess whether the hundreds of billions of dollars being poured into AI infrastructure will generate good returns.

The selloff, which began before the U.S. midterm elections, intensified after Democrats scored sweeping victories on Tuesday, raising concerns that political opposition could slow the industry's expansion.

The shifting sentiment comes at a key moment for AI model lab Anthropic, which is preparing for a potential IPO that could become one of the biggest tests yet of public-market appetite for AI companies. Goldman is among the banks leading that IPO process.

"We'll see, obviously, if this IPO moves forward, how it all plays out," Solomon said, declining to comment in detail about the IPO. "I would say that the market environment is still pretty constructive."

Solomon also pushed back against political resistance to data center construction, arguing that the infrastructure is essential to the U.S. economy and that it can be built responsibly.

"We certainly don't want this infrastructure going to places outside the U.S., which is what will naturally happen because the demand for compute is there," Solomon said.

#459. The Hubble Space Telescope Is Sinking. NASA Is Looking for Ways to Keep It Afloat

As telescopes go, few if any are more famous than the Hubble Space Telescope. Over 30 years of operations, the telescope has been essential to groundbreaking discoveries that have fundamentally transformed humanity’s view of the universe. But like all satellites in low Earth orbit, it’s been gradually descending.

This happens because of atmospheric drag, and the Hubble Space Telescope is not equipped with a propulsion system to adjust its own orbit. According to NASA’s analysis, if left unchecked, the telescope will likely re-enter Earth’s atmosphere in 2033 or 2034, eventually crashing back onto the planet.

Hoping to delay this conclusion, NASA has issued a formal Request for Information seeking proposals from the private sector for “in-space servicing” of the famous telescope. This would likely involve raising Hubble’s altitude to extend its operational lifespan, but the agency is also open to industry’s suggestions for managing a safe and controlled reentry or disposal of the telescope while still in orbit. Space companies can also propose upgrades to enhance the telescope’s functionality in its remaining years.

“Hubble is a national icon,” Nicky Fox, associate administrator of NASA's Science Mission Directorate, said in a statement. “Given the time we have until Hubble’s anticipated reentry, we are eager to explore the breadth of possibilities to boost Hubble to a higher orbit while we develop the flagship mission to follow.”

The telescope was designed with the expectation that astronauts would perform repairs and upgrades in orbit. Since it was launched in 1990, a total of five service missions have been carried out. The last of these missions was canceled after the space shuttle Columbia disaster in 2003. But NASA later determined that the benefits of servicing Hubble outweighed the risks of a final mission, and in 2009 a crew took measures, such as replacing the battery, to extend the telescope’s lifespan.

This is also not the first time NASA has considered private-sector resources for raising the Hubble Space Telescope’s orbit. In September 2022, NASA entered into a nonfunding partnership agreement with SpaceX to investigate the possibility of using the company’s Dragon spacecraft to move Hubble into a more stable orbit. However, according to NASA’s announcement at the time, the agency had no plans to conduct or fund the service mission itself; the study was intended solely to assess commercial feasibility.

Meanwhile, NASA commissioned the private company Katalyst Space Technologies in September 2025 to perform an orbital raise for the Swift Observatory, which was experiencing faster-than-expected orbital decay. However, the company’s spacecraft, launched in July 2026, suffered an attitude control malfunction, forcing the mission to be abandoned. On November 4, Swift reentered the atmosphere over Indonesia.

The problems facing the Hubble Space Telescope are not limited to its drop in altitude. The spacecraft itself is also experiencing malfunctions. In June 2024, NASA announced that it would transition the Hubble Space Telescope to a new operating mode using only one of the six gyros on board to assess the telescope’s orientation. After more than three decades in space, only three of the gyros were operational, and one of those was starting to return abnormal readings. The other remaining functional gyro is now being kept in a ready state for future use.

NASA estimates that a mission to raise the telescope’s orbit will need to be carried out by 2032. In an announcement made in 2022, NASA also explained that if the Hubble Space Telescope could be raised to a higher, more stable orbit, it could continue to observe the cosmos for several more years.

“Hubble still has important science ahead of it, and we should be looking at every reasonable opportunity to keep it delivering for as long as possible,” said NASA administrator Jared Isaacman. “If there are innovative and cost-effective ways to extend the life of an asset already in orbit, we owe it to the American taxpayer and the scientific community to explore them. Extending Hubble’s life would mean more discoveries, more science, and more opportunities to expand our understanding of the universe.”

This story was originally published in WIRED Japan and has been translated from Japanese.

#458. Boeing to cut 5% of commercial airplane workers as production increase takes longer than expected

Boeing said Monday that it plans to cut 5% of its commercial airplane workforce as its production ramp-up is moving at a slower pace than the company expected.

The cuts will be through a combination of attrition and layoffs, Stephanie Pope, head of the commercial aircraft unit, said in a memo to staff.

Pope reiterated that the company is still working to stabilize production rates of its bestselling 737 Max planes, which are made outside of Seattle.

CEO Kelly Ortberg told investors last month that the company won't significantly increase production of the Max aircraft until the second half of next year. He said the company's wing production has been slower than expected.

"Adjusting our production schedules and reducing aircraft output puts us in an overstaffed position," Pope said in her note Monday. "Unfortunately, that means we need to align our staffing levels to our revised business plan, resulting in a reduction of approximately 5% through a combination of attrition and involuntary layoffs, with notifications beginning in December."

Boeing is targeting a production rate of 52 planes a month in the second half of 2027. Ortberg said the company has been trying to stabilize output at a rate of about 47 of its 737 Max planes a month.

#454. Microsoft global outage: What the company has said so far

Updated on Friday, Nov. 13 at 11:55 a.m. ET — Microsoft provided a statement to Mashable expressing "regret" for the outage. The company also said it didn't believe a cyberattack caused the outage.

"We’re seeing encouraging recovery progress, with service availability and email delivery performance improving across a growing portion of affected infrastructure. We know our customers rely on our services to get important work done every day, and regret the impact these outages had on those who depend on us. Our teams are reviewing what happened and how we can prevent similar issues going forward." — Charles Lamanna, EVP, Copilot, Agents and Platform at Microsoft

Updated on Friday, Nov. 13 at 4:59 a.m. ET — Microsoft says it's "seeing early stabilization and capacity improvements as recovery actions continue." The company also said it has extended email timeout to 48 hours to help preserve delayed mail during this event.

Updated on Friday, Nov. 13 at 1:08 a.m. ET — Microsoft is still looking into the cause of the outage, but states that at present it is "not aware of any malicious activity that is causing this incident."

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 10:58 p.m. ET — Unfortunately, Microsoft's efforts to bring servers back online aren't going as planned. It appears to have been unable to address the issue of them becoming unresponsive again, and has stated it is now searching for different ways to increase traffic capacity.

"We've monitored the status of the changes applied to improve serviceability and confirmed they're not taking as expected," Microsoft wrote in an update on X.

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 8:07 p.m. ET — Microsoft has revised its list of features and services impacted by this outage, stating that it is affecting ones that interact with Exchange Online and email services. Microsoft Outlook, SharePoint Online, Teams, Copilot AI, and 365 are all continuing to have various issues, though Azure appears to be operational.

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 7:53 p.m. ET — Microsoft has issued a new update stating that the servers they've brought back online may become unresponsive again when they receive traffic. The company is currently investigating the issue, though in the meantime it's limiting the types of traffic going to these servers in some locations.

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 6:23 p.m. ET — Microsoft states that it is bringing some servers back online, and "they remain stable so far." Users should expect a "gradual recovery."

"We're also shifting traffic to alternate routes wherever possible and observing limited recovery," Microsoft wrote in an update on X. "In parallel, we've streamlined the process of returning the remaining affected servers to service."

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 5:39 p.m. ET — As of this writing, the global Microsoft outage remains ongoing.

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 3:55 p.m. ET — We've updated this story with the latest statements from Microsoft.

Starting in the early afternoon, Microsoft 365 enterprise software products experienced a major outage, which extended into Friday morning. On the platform DownDetector, more than 100,000 users reported problems with Outlook alone, starting around 12:30 p.m. ET on Thursday.

The company has posted regular updates about the situation, both on social media and on its website.

On the company's official status page (which also went offline briefly, according to multiple Mashable reporters), Microsoft said that Exchange Online, Teams, SharePoint Online, Copilot 365, Defender XDR, and other services were experiencing issues. At first, the status page indicated that the outage only affected Microsoft's enterprise software, Microsoft 365; however, as of this writing, the page lists Microsoft Consumer Products as also experiencing "service degradation."

On Friday morning, the company finally provided an official statement about the global outage. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

"We’re seeing encouraging recovery progress, with service availability and email delivery performance improving across a growing portion of affected infrastructure," said Charles Lamanna, EVP, Copilot, Agents and Platform at Microsoft. "We know our customers rely on our services to get important work done every day, and regret the impact these outages had on those who depend on us. Our teams are reviewing what happened and how we can prevent similar issues going forward."

And in an update posted to X, the company said it had no reason to believe a cyberattack was the cause of the outage.

"We will continue to share more information as we have it, and for now we can confirm that there is no indication this is the result of malicious or cyber activity. Our teams are reviewing what happened and how we can prevent similar issues going forward."

In an initial update posted at 12:54 p.m. ET to X, the company said that customers were having problems with Exchange Online mailboxes "in the Americas." Exchange Online is a cloud-based tool that provides back-end support for Microsoft Outlook.

However, by 1:07 p.m. ET, the company acknowledged the problem was more widespread, affecting more services and regions. "While we're working to mitigate impact, we've confirmed the issue may affect Microsoft 365 service connectivity for users in any region," the company posted.

The Microsoft Status page was then updated with a notification that stated, "User impact: Users may be unable to connect to multiple Microsoft 365 services."

While a root cause has yet to be determined, Microsoft has at least shared a brief technical explanation for what's going on.

"We've determined that the elevated load isn't driven by an overall increase in traffic, but by a large number of servers being unexpectedly unavailable, which is shifting load onto the remaining infrastructure," Microsoft said on its status page, as well as in a thread on X. "We're investigating what's causing these servers to become unavailable while we work to safely return them to service."

Later on in the X thread, Microsoft said it had not identified any recent change that correlates with the outage, but "the failed health checks may originate from a specific component that's responsible for processing server health." In the meantime, Microsoft has worked to prevent more servers from being impacted while it also tries to resuscitate the ones that are down.

Shortly before 4 p.m. ET, the Microsoft status page received another update, stating that it is still troubleshooting and restarting servers.

"While validating the targeted change, we found that many of the affected servers have become unresponsive, which is preventing us from confirming whether the change is effective," Microsoft said. "We're restarting these servers so they can come back online with the change in place, and we're applying the same approach to additional affected portions of our infrastructure to validate the results more broadly."

By the evening, Microsoft's updates turned slightly more optimistic, as the company stated that some servers had already come back online. A 4:00 p.m. update stated that the company had "validated an alternative service health check method, which allowed a subset of affected servers to successfully return to service."

However, by 5:09 p.m. on Thursday, the company acknowledged that services remained offline.

"We’re opening all available pathways to restore service, as our actions taken thus far have not provided the anticipated relief," read an update posted to its website.

As the outage continued into Friday morning, the company finally reported progress toward recovery.

#452. ‘Call Him Out’: Cybertruck Loyalists Are in Mourning—and They Want Answers From Elon Musk

Last week, Elon Musk announced on X that Tesla would halt production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas “around the middle of next year,” supposedly to allow for increased production of self-driving Cybercabs.

Automotive industry observers were quick to surmise that the vehicle’s weak sales, delayed deliveries, inflated pricing, numerous recalls, and connection to Musk’s far-right MAGA politics may also have factored into the decision. Longtime detractors of the polarizing steel-paneled truck, originally positioned as an electric rival to classic gas-powered pickups, jeered Musk and Tesla fans over its demise, noting with some pleasure that the Cybertruck would exit the market within four years of its November 2023 rollout.

And, in Cybertruck fan communities across the web, owners began to mourn.

“Worst possible news,” a member of the Cybertruck Owners Club forum wrote in a response to Musk’s post. “No chance to buy another in future years for a newer model or after a car accident. No more aftermarket mods. No more service or parts.” Another remarked that they had waited for their truck “longer than the truck line was around.”

“So everybody in my group, they are not happy,” Michael Goldman, a contractor in Palm Springs, California, tells WIRED. As the administrator of the Facebook group Cybertruck Owners Only, which has more than 82,000 members, he’s been swamped with frantic messages. “I've had people reaching out to me through the group, asking me, like, ‘What are you going to do?’” Goldman says. “And I'm like, ‘Dude, I don't know.’”

Since hearing the news about the discontinuation of the truck, many owners have expressed feelings of betrayal and disappointment. Some are wondering if their pending orders will be fulfilled, as Musk has claimed that Tesla “will have to return deposits for a lot of people” due to demand outpacing production. Others have looked for a silver lining, speculating that the Cybertruck could become a valued collector’s item.

But Goldman doesn’t want to turn his truck into a “garage princess,” since he enjoys driving it and finds it useful in his construction business. “I don't want to ever drive another truck,” he says, and he considered buying a backup Cybertruck for when his first one reaches the end of its life. “I'll be honest with you, I was going pull the trigger on a [Cyberbeast] about two days ago, and I have a reservation already in my phone,” Goldman says, but when he was ready to complete the purchase, “the price increased like another $8,000 or something, and that was my sign to not do it.”

That Musk, against all odds, actually brought his vision of the Cybertruck to life makes it all the more painful for Goldman to see it scrapped. “That it actually happened and only happened for such a short period of time kind of blows me away,” he says. “That's why I want to call him out on it, you know?”

Troy Meekhof, a Tesla aficionado in Grand Rapids, Michigan, known as “the Cybertruck Guy” on social media, has not been shy about expressing his own displeasure. “I can’t help but think this is Tesla’s own unwillingness (or incompetence) to make Cybertruck a success,” he posted in reply to Musk’s announcement of the wind-down last week. “Where was the price cuts? Where were the advertisements? Where is range extender?”

“I absolutely do think this is the wrong move for the Tesla brand, and it’s not necessarily because of the removal of an under-performing product, but it’s a clear symbol of Tesla’s aggression towards transitioning to autonomous vehicles only,” such as the two-seater Cybercab, Meekhof tells WIRED in an email. “If you ask me, it is far too aggressive, and I have a growing conviction that, at least in America, the population and legislators are not ready nor interested in autonomous vehicles today en masse.”

In another X post, Meekhof warned that “Tesla has lost the plot” by going all-in on autonomous driving as the US enters “a governance era that opposes” the company’s efforts on that front.

Musk has repeatedly declared that Tesla’s future is as an AI and robotics firm rather than as an automaker. It’s a strategy that has increasingly alienated Tesla owners like Meekhof, who despite their excitement over self-driving technology, feel that the company is spurning its core supporters as it pursues breakthroughs in that field. Earlier this year, Tesla axed its Model S and Model X, and the end of the Cybertruck narrows its lineup to just the Model 3 and Model Y.

“Tesla, as a company, has made itself massively successful by making vehicles that appeal to enthusiasts, in either tech or automotive, and in many cases, both,” Meekhof says. “With their transition to autonomous-only vehicles, they leave those customers behind. I am one of them.” Meekhof has launched what he describes as a mostly “symbolic” petition urging Tesla to reconsider the plan to eliminate the Cybertruck from its slate. The letter has gathered more than 5,000 signatures.

Drew Million, an active member of the Cybertruck Owners Only group on Facebook, recently shared on the page that he was concerned about the transition to another vehicle once his beloved Cybertruck has run its course. “I’ve lost a lot of love for Tesla over this,” Million tells WIRED, adding that his interest in the brand is waning due to the “very stale consumer options” left on the table.

Million had ordered a second Cybertruck in February to replace a work van, and he’s hoping that it comes through before production halts completely in June or July 2027, per Musk’s estimate. But he’s dismayed, he says, that Tesla leadership isn’t paying attention to what customers actually want.

“After Cybertruck is gone, there isn’t a flagship vehicle,” Million says, nor one that meets his needs. His household went from owning no electric cars to four in less than a year. “The Cybertruck started that,” he says, and it can’t be replaced by rideshares in autonomous taxis.

It’s a point that comes up again and again from Tesla boosters protesting the sunset of the Cybertruck: They don’t want to give up their own independence in favor of robot chauffeurs. An owner wrote on X, for example, that they didn’t understand Tesla’s push “to delete the steering wheel, the pedals, the ownership, the freedom of having a vehicle” with the Cybercab, which cannot accommodate an entire family. They lamented that the company “doesn't seem to want to continue innovating in the customer car segment.”

Meekhof predicts that Americans won’t soon give up personally controlled trucks and SUVs capable of transporting several people and cargo in favor of smaller autonomous sedans. He worries that Tesla is headed for a “Google Glass scenario on steroids, where the technology came before the people.” Goldman is on the same page. “I think a Cybercab is cool for certain things, but I'm not going to take a Cybercab to a construction site, you know what I mean?” he says. “In rural towns, people want to drive their car to work.”

The overall consternation in Tesla world over the fate of Cybertruck has been somewhat compounded by Musk’s typically muddled messaging. According to the CEO, the truck is profitable and, indeed, so popular that the Gigafactory can’t make them fast enough to fulfill existing reservations—so why, owners ask, does it have to be suspended?

Would-be customers have also been disgruntled to learn that Tesla continues to accept Cybertruck orders, though only for the premium Cyberbeast model, priced at $109,990. “So they can't fill current orders, but will still take orders on the most expensive version ... yeah that's not scummy at all,” a redditor griped this week on r/cybertruck.

And there is a percolating sense of resentment among some Cybertruck devotees that they were taken for a ride, so to speak, with Tesla exploiting their passion for the novel vehicle and its driver-assistance tech to advance its agenda of autonomy at all costs.

“They're a data company,” Goldman says. “What they're doing is collecting data for the robots and for the self-driving cars and for the Cybercab. We were basically beta testers in the Cybertruck for the Cybercab.”

That’s part of the reason that Goldman and others feel they are owed a better explanation of Tesla’s latest move. “We want to know why,” Goldman says. “Is it because we were really just beta for the cabs, or is it really because you guys don't have enough space in the Gigafactory, or is it just because the sales are terrible?”

“Most of the people that have Cybertrucks have been following this truck for the past 10 years,” Goldman says. “We finally got literally the best truck made on this planet, and now they're taking it away from us, and we're bummed.”

We sexted with a Muse AI agent—and Meta says that’s totally fine

Big Story: Meet the DEA’s premier crypto detective

Want better search results? Make WIRED a preferred source in Google

#451. PepsiCo to cut about 1,000 jobs as North American business struggles

PepsiCo is cutting about 1,000 jobs across North America as the company tries to turn around lagging sales in its home market.

"We have a clear ambition for PepsiCo North America: to build a stronger, more future-fit business that delivers industry-leading growth," Ram Krishnan, CEO of PepsiCo North America, wrote in a Monday letter to employees obtained by CNBC. "To do that, we need to be faster, more connected, and more capable, while continuing to invest in growth — behind our people, capabilities and brands."

Over the next week, Pepsi plans to cut jobs as it says it aims to streamline its operations and better connect teams across the company's North American business. PepsiCo employs about 306,000 people worldwide, including roughly 125,000 in the United States, as of Dec. 27, according to a company filing.

The Wall Street Journal first reported the coming layoffs.

In October, PepsiCo cut its full-year earnings outlook, citing the struggles in its home market. During the third quarter, its North American beverage unit saw volume shrink 2%, while its North American food division reported flat volume.

While the company has tried to boost sales by cutting prices and investing in marketing, the turnaround is taking longer than expected, Chief Financial Officer Steve Schmitt said on the company's earnings conference call.

Shares of Pepsi rose slightly in afternoon trading. The stock is down nearly 1% this year, lagging behind the S&P 500's gains of nearly 11% over the same period.

#449. Children’s Place files for bankruptcy protection, seeks buyer

Children's clothing retailer The Children's Place has filed for chapter 11 bankruptcy protection, it said Sunday.

The company is looking to sell substantially all of its assets and is seeking offers from potential buyers by Dec. 9, with a goal to close a deal by the end of December.

The retailer, whose brands include The Children's Place and Gymboree, says it operates two digital storefronts and 514 North American stores, and has distribution in 13 countries through franchise and wholesale partners. The company's performance has taken a hit in recent years from tariff hikes and slower consumer spending, among other factors.

Its stores and e-commerce sites remain open for now, and the company is planning to continue its ongoing inventory sales with court approval.

"For nearly 60 years, The Children's Place has supported growing families with apparel that customers have come to know and love. We recognize the significance of this development for our employees, customers, vendors, and other partners, and we are committed to conducting an efficient and transparent sale process," said Rudy Morando, co-chief restructuring officer of The Children's Place. "In the meantime, we remain focused on serving the families who have trusted The Children's Place for generations."

This move comes days after interim Children's Place CEO Muhammad Asif Seemab and Chief Strategy Officer Muhammad Umair stepped down from their roles. Seemab and Umair will both remain on the company's board. CFO John Szczepanski took on the role of acting president and principal executive officer.

Seemab is also managing director of Saudi-based Mithaq Capital, which took a majority stake in The Children's Place in 2024 when the company was facing financial trouble.

Even with support from the investors, the company has not been able to significantly improve its financial outlook. In its bankruptcy filing, the company reported about $742 million in assets, compared to about $881 million in debts.

In the second fiscal quarter of 2026, its most recently reported quarter, the company's net sales dropped nearly 19% from the same period the year prior. At the time, Seemab said in a news release that during the quarter the company was "stabilizing our customer file" and "driving traffic to our stores and websites," and saw "lower conversion, combined with a very clearance-heavy mix."

He also pointed to tough comparisons to the prior-year period, due in part to back-to-school shopping demand that "was uniquely elevated ... ahead of anticipated tariff price increases."

The Children's Place stock has plummeted in recent years and was trading around 18 cents per share on Monday.

#445. Inside the 42-Year Journey to Bring ‘Neuromancer’ to the Screen

Briana Middleton has something to show me. “Wait!” she says just as we’re about to end our Zoom call, reaching for something just beyond her screen.

“When I found out we were going to be doing this,” she says, “I was at the thrift shop in my neighborhood and I found this.” Then she points it at the camera: an issue of WIRED from 1995 featuring Keanu Reeves on the cover alongside the line “William Gibson on the making of Johnny Mnemonic.”

Very apropos. The 1995 film Johnny Mnemonic was based on a short story by Gibson, who also wrote the screenplay. Molly, the “razorgirl” Middleton plays in Apple TV’s forthcoming Neuromancer adaptation, made her first appearance in that short story. Her relationship with the protagonist Case, played in the series by Callum Turner, mirrors the one Reeves’ Neo had with Trinity in The Matrix, which is steeped in Gibsoneque lore.

For Middleton, to be giving an interview to WIRED about Neuromancer’s epic journey to the screen not long after finding a 1995 issue of the magazine in a secondhand store is so uncanny it almost feels like a glitch. “Dude, it was eight bucks,” she says, agreeing that eight dollars is a steal for a vintage WIRED. “I was like, ‘This is crazy.’”

Maybe all things happen exactly when they’re supposed to. Gibson originally released Neuromancer in 1984, back before hackers were cool. Back before hackers were CEOs. It focused on Case, a young computer jockey who can get online through a jack in his head. He teams up with Molly and a former military man named Armitage to break into the offworld home of the Tessier-Ashpool family, who became astoundingly rich thanks to the tech company they founded.

The book’s novel ideas—cyberspace, brain-computer interfaces, all-powerful AIs—were years away from being mainstream, let alone possible. In the years since, there’s been much talk of turning Neuromancer into a movie or TV show, but it never took off, even as Hollywood mined sci-fi for hits.

On January 22, all of that will change when Apple TV releases its long-anticipated adaptation. Ahead of its release, WIRED got in touch with Middleton, Turner, and creators Graham Roland and JD Dillard to find out how they became the first to bring the book to screens—and the burden of taking on one of sci-fi’s classics.

Graham Roland: It's a huge seminal book. I had not read it until the summer of 2020, but I was aware it was one of those white whales. It inspired a lot of things, and people had been trying to adapt it for years.

Callum Turner: The world will be taken on a wild ride. It’s an epic. It’s Blade Runner; it’s 2001: A Space Odyssey; it’s The Fifth Element. This is the first time it comes onto our screens, and that's a big moment for us and fans of sci-fi.

Briana Middleton: When we were shooting it and in the thick of it, it was easier to not get freaked out by the expectations. It felt nice to be in a little bubble with the world that we had created. I tried to look at the expectations that people clearly have and the love that people have for this book and what these characters mean. I definitely get freaked out sometimes and I just have to shut my computer and not think about it.

Roland: I had a meeting at Skydance, and they had given me the book and said, “Hey, the rights of this book are going to come available again. We’re going to try to adapt it as a TV show as opposed to a movie or several movies." It made so much sense. So I read the book, and really responded to it right away. JD and I had been friends for years prior to that, and were looking for a way to work together. He had also had a meeting at Skydance, and they had also given him the book.

JD Dillard: Yeah, there was a wonderful kismet of this moment, and we were like, “Wait, we’re both talking to people about this. Why don’t we do it together?”

Dillard: Drake has always been a fan of Neuromancer and as a storyteller has great taste, but that's only part of it. The real magic is that he’s built an incredible team at [his production company] DreamCrew that he trusts to bring things to life once he identifies something he loves or believes in. Jason Shrier, who leads film and TV for DreamCrew, is one of the folks in our little brain trust on this show, and someone we pride as always having his finger on the pulse. But he has a relationship with [entertainment company] Anonymous Content, and Skydance, so it was kind of serendipitous that as Drake and DreamCrew had identified the books, all of these parties were starting to form and realize that they could work together to go get the rights. Drake, individually and by way of DreamCrew, is part of the mix and remains an important part of our process.

Roland: The way it was set up was that William Gibson was going to be the decider of who was going to get the rights when they came available. So we went to work on a pitch and with the hope that we would be the people he would choose.

We had heard that there were some bigger names, much bigger than JD and I. I just felt like there was a counterculture vibe about him and I was like, I don't think it matters that we're nobodies. I think if we can somehow communicate our passion for his book, and not just go and say, “Oh, obviously we’ll take care of your book, you can trust us,” but actually go in and do the work and come up with a thoughtful presentation and a thoughtful pitch, then I think we've got as good a shot as any.

Dillard: I'm sure Bill would not mind us sharing this part of it, but it's very easy to look at these big pieces of literature or movies that have stood the test of time and just think that they were immaculately conceived. Just to hear him talk about where he struggled and what he was self-conscious about and then what he was surprised worked and what he wished he could have done, what he wished he had time to do—it was the first time Graham and I both looked at Neuromancer as a living text.

Dillard: One of the first questions we asked ourselves was “How do you make this feel new in spite of inspiring so many things that have now already come out ahead of this?” It was never going to be our ambition to out-Cyberpunk 2077 Cyberpunk 2077. But what we could do is talk to Gibson and find out where, for him, some of those big tonal touchpoints are. An interesting one that came up really early in talking to him was that he had never perceived the world of Neuromancer as a dystopia. Even that was a nice thing to just remember as we found our way through it.

The best we could do is bring people to the experience of what it felt like reading it for the first time in 1984. Because anything beyond that, your reference points are already going to be derivative. If you put up Blade Runner reference art, you're already behind it. The joke that we were even making when hiring [production designer] Luke [Hull] was like: Here's someone who's done Chernobyl and Andor, and in our minds Neuromancer is Chernobyl plus Andor. It's old-school and hi-fi and colliding those things together.

Roland: We didn't want the world of Neuromancer to feel like X amount of years after the world that we were living in. In the teaser that opens the show, you see this piece of technology in the late '60s, early '70s that we know did not exist back then in our world. We wanted to announce right out of the gate: Don't be looking for the iteration of whatever phone you have in your pocket 50 years from now. We want this to be its own world.

Dillard: Watching someone hack will only be exciting for so long. But what is stakes-driven is the danger to Case in doing it and the toll it takes on his body. Is he going to get the thing they need for the larger story for himself? Can he prove to himself that he can still do it? All of those things were immediately stickier than just watching him do it.

A thing Bill [Gibson] said really early on is, he had always sort of imagined Case as a neurological athlete. We took that and ran with it, in that it should take a toll. There's an expenditure to do this work.

Turner: I try not to think about how important it is and focus on my job of playing the scenes in front of me. JD and Graham had William Gibson’s approval, the scripts were brilliant, and the world so detailed and real. You can smell this world, which feels unique.

Roland: I'm so glad that one of the first things we did was go see Bill Gibson up in Vancouver. He took the burden off of us and let us know that he wasn't expecting a literal translation of his text. He's the one that we're really beholden to.

Even in those early conversations with him, he mentioned, “Oh, I always wanted to do a bigger Tessier-Ashpool story, the story of the family [behind the powerful AI called Wintermute]. I had a book deadline, and I never really got to expand on that story. But if you find yourself looking for other things, other liberties or places to go into and make your own, that would be one.” So that's why we came up with that storyline.

Roland: Before we ever got to hiring the heads of any departments, JD planted a flag and said, “As much as we can, we want an in-camera aesthetic. We want to be practical.” That really drove the decision to go to Japan, because trying to recreate Chiba City, we already have set extensions, obviously, because it takes place in the future, but the textures of Japan [are there], and we wanted it to feel like you were actually there.

The other thing we did, the thing I had never done on any other show, was we hired our production designer first, before any other head of a department. We knew that while we were still writing we needed that person to help us realize the world. What they were going to do practically—from a props perspective, from a concept art perspective—was going to inform our writing as well.

Dillard: It is a very bespoke version of the future; everything had to be machined and built and designed. We didn’t have all of these things that you could normally just go to the prop shop and get. But that is what drives the feeling of Neuromancer, feeling textured and grounded and real and a place you could go to, and it is both familiar to the past but aspirational to the future.

On the design of Case’s deck, the terminal he uses to access the virtual world of the matrix:

Dillard: We saw a lot of decks. If I remember correctly, I feel like we were on the way to being like, "OK, this is the deck." Then Luke was like, "Sorry, before we make that, here's another one." That is the one we went with. But we were all resistant to it just feeling like a laptop. We wanted something that was a little bit like a station.

On getting the synth-rock band Son Lux for the score:

Dillard: Graham and I knew from the beginning we wanted Neuromancer to feel different, not feel cyberpunk. In the same way that every other department had to honor retro-futurism in their own way, we wanted to charge our composer with doing the same.

They're the best, and a band we've been fans of for a long time. I don't know how many Son Lux concerts I've gone to, and it was really cool in the last few years to see their turn into scores. Obviously, Everything Everywhere All at Once was a big one for them. Ryan [Lott] and the gang just really found Neuromancer's own sonic signature.

Roland: We had finished two or three scripts, and we started having conversations about casting. Apple had had a great experience with Callum on Masters of the Air and they encouraged us to meet with him. It was the first and only meeting we had. He was in LA for something else and he came to meet JD and I, and within five minutes of hearing his voice it was like, “This is the guy.”

Turner: My vision was aligned with theirs, and luckily they asked me to join. I hadn’t read the book, but I was in a sci-fi mode. I’d read I Am Legend and Do Androids Dream of Electric Sheep? when this came through. I remember we had [Neuromancer] in our house when I was a kid, but I’d never picked it up. Graham and JD had written a wonderful character, and he felt so nuanced and detailed on the page—I jumped off from their starting point.

Case has the right mix of dangerous, courageous, and naive. Joe Strummer was a big influence. Thom Yorke. Iggy Pop. There’s an incredible documentary called Streetwise that I used as a base. There are always many, many things that go into a pot.

On maybe casting the actor who will be the next James Bond:

Dillard: We're crossing our fingers and toes.

Dillard: We had Case, so it was about “What completes the duo?” It’s a vibe thing; it’s a physicality thing. Callum's quite tall. And not that our shorter friends can't kick ass too, but you just wanted it to make sense on screen. Callum was shooting in Vancouver, and we brought a few people to read across from him. It was Briana for sure. They just had such a fun chemistry, and she just had the kind of no-nonsense grit to her. She could find these neat moments of warmth, but also not have that break her hard shell. I know everybody says this in their interviews about their show, but we really lucked out.

Middleton: I was not familiar with the book, and I had a meeting with David Levine at Anonymous Content. When he initially told me about the character and the fact that she's got lenses over her eyes, I was like, “I don't think I want to do that. That doesn't sound fun at all.” Then I met with JD and Graham months later, and they just sold me on it.

I feel like Molly is very funny in our show, and in the book, Molly is very cool, and there's a lot that we don't see that is very clearly there. Molly is a guarded character, but there is so much going on, and we do get to see some more of the very human part of her outside of the “razorgirl” badass trope.

Middleton: They were stuck on my face. It was double-sided sticky tape basically, but they held on. I think they fell off maybe once or twice throughout the entire shoot, and that includes fight scenes, so they held up. Steps were always a daunting thing. I couldn't really tell when I was going to step off something or be fine.

Whenever I took them off, it would feel weird. I would feel kind of exposed. I think at some point in the shoot, they started to feel probably how Molly feels, that they are very comforting and very much like, I can hide a little bit.

On having the jack—a brain-computer interface—attached to the back of your neck:

On William Gibson’s cameo as a patron at the Chatsubo dive bar:

Roland: We invited him to the set in London. I knew we had a lot of fans of his book working on our crew, but it wasn’t until he arrived that I saw the number of people that had brought their personal copy of Neuromancer hoping to get it signed. I was like, “Oh wow, I guess they're not here for the scripts.” [Laughs]

Dillard: I think we were like, “We want him to get out here, and we know it's a long slog from Vancouver, but if we can get him out here, we got to put him in. We have to just immortalize his presence in the show.” So part of it was timing, but it actually worked out for what we wanted because Chatsubo is such an iconic location in the book, and to see him there as a barfly just was like, “This will be so fun.”

On whether the other books in William Gibson’s Sprawl Trilogy—Count Zero and Mona Lisa Overdrive—could find their way onto the small screen:

Roland: It's too early to say at this point. I think we're still kind of focused on getting this one out into the world, but we'll see. I hope so.

On tech entrepreneurs being inspired by Neuromancer:

Dillard: There's some great tweet that went viral last year that is sort of like, "New tech CEO says they're launching a product called Skynet.” It's like you're missing the point if you're naming the thing after the bad thing.

Middleton: Our show is asking what happens whenever corporations have unchecked power and when a technology is unchecked and there's no regulations, and our relationship with that technology goes unchecked. Our show is asking what we lose without oversight. I think life gets much, much worse. It's scary. I'm scared.

#443. Lucid misses Wall Street’s earnings expectations, expands loan from Saudi Arabia fund

Lucid Group missed Wall Street's third-quarter expectations as the embattled electric vehicle maker continues an "operational reset" aimed at cutting costs and narrowing losses.

The company on Monday also announced a $1.75 billion increase in a delayed draw term loan from Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund, the company's largest shareholder.

Lucid stock fell roughly 5% in aftermarket trading Monday.

Here's how Lucid performed in the third quarter compared with average estimates compiled by LSEG:

Despite the top- and bottom-line misses, the company's adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of a loss of $726 million beat Wall Street's expectations of a loss of $611 million for the third quarter. Its net loss for the quarter was $830.9 million, down from a loss of $978.4 million a year earlier.

Lucid's 2025 third quarter included revenue of $336.6 million, a loss of $3.31 per share and an adjusted EBITDA loss of $717.7 million.

The quarterly results are the first since Lucid CEO Silvio Napoli, who started leading the company in June, announced a restructuring plan that included implementing several cost-cutting efforts and delaying the company's upcoming midsize vehicle from the end of this year until the second half of next.

"Our transformation is beginning to show progress. We are acting with urgency, strengthening the fundamentals and driving greater discipline, while continuing to invest in the priorities to deliver profitable long-term growth," Napoli said in a release.

The company's turnaround plan includes $1.4 billion in cash flow improvements this year as well as focusing attention on a robotaxi program with Uber and Nuro, a factory that's under construction in Saudi Arabia and the midsize vehicle.

Lucid said the capital from PIF is expected to give the company "additional financial flexibility as Lucid delivers on its turnaround program." It is expected to assist in funding "operations and key investments" into the fourth quarter of 2027, including the start of production for the midsize vehicle.

The additional funds boost Lucid's total liquidity to $3.75 billion, up from $2 billion to end the third quarter.

"We are grateful for the continued financial support from our anchor shareholder, which further energizes us to remain focused on execution and on our commitments as we build a stronger, sustainable Lucid," Napoli said.

Lucid said Monday that the company expects to communicate its midterm plan and targets early next year. The EV maker in May suspended its guidance and many targets, citing new leadership and time needed to develop a turnaround plan.

#441. Microsoft outage reactions: The world woke up with Outlook still down. The memes are incredible.

Updated on Friday, Nov. 13 at 10:22 a.m. ET — Outlook service appears to be gradually returning for some users. Some U.S. users took to social media to lament the fact that they're able to do their jobs again.

Microsoft's major global outage seems like it's winding down, as of Friday morning. The issue wasn't solved overnight, and some Microsoft users in the UK woke up with broken inboxes — and got to work making memes. However, by the time Americans woke up and started their Friday, service was returning to normal.

Users worldwide started reporting issues with Microsoft 365 enterprise software products, including Outlook, Teams, and Copilot, around 5:30 p.m. GMT on Nov. 12, according to Downdetector. At the time of writing, major Microsoft services are still experiencing problems, per Microsoft's official status page, but the company is seeing "sustained improvements in service availability."

Fortunately (or unfortunately, depending on how you look at it), that meant many American users had to get back to work on Friday morning. Social media was full of appropriate memes, as a result.

People in the UK were not as lucky, as services were still broadly offline when they woke up. UK users hit social media to react to the news and touch grass like the rest of the world (or for serious information-sharing purposes).

Schools and universities, NHS emergency services, pharmacies, GPs, and care services, transport, charities, businesses, museums, and government offices around the country have been affected by the outage. Surgical procedures have had to be rescheduled, per Bloomberg. The Metropolitan Police website currently says that "some online form submissions may be affected" due to the Microsoft outage. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

The Royal Mail, however, took a moment to be smug.

Microsoft is regularly updating its official status page and social media channels, and Mashable is covering the story as it happens.

On Friday, in response to Mashable's questions about the outage, Microsoft provided a statement expressing regret for the disruption.

"We’re seeing encouraging recovery progress, with service availability and email delivery performance improving across a growing portion of affected infrastructure," said Charles Lamanna, EVP, Copilot, Agents and Platform at Microsoft. "We know our customers rely on our services to get important work done every day, and regret the impact these outages had on those who depend on us. Our teams are reviewing what happened and how we can prevent similar issues going forward."

#439. ‘A Servant Class, Not a Working Class’: Stephen Miller’s Biographer on What Comes Next

Last week, President Donald Trump named Stephen Miller, his longtime adviser and architect of the administration’s widely unpopular immigration agenda, his new chief of staff. With the midterm elections delivering a Democratic majority in both the Senate and the House of Representatives, Miller, whose titles until now were deputy chief of staff for policy and homeland security adviser, will likely play a critical role in shaping the White House’s agenda over the next two years.

There is probably no one who understands Miller better than journalist Jean Guerrero. During Trump’s first administration, Guerrero became fascinated by Miller, who, like her, had grown up in Southern California, surrounded by immigrants, in largely liberal (or at least not hard-line conservative) communities. How, she wondered, did this man, whose family came to the US fleeing antisemitic pogroms, who grew up in close proximity to immigrants, come to align himself with white supremacist and anti-immigrant views?

Her research culminated in Hatemonger, her 2020 biography of Miller, in which she charted his rapid ascent to power in the first Trump administration and dove into the ideas, mentors, and experiences that formed the foundations of Miller’s beliefs.

“Stephen Miller has been a key player in delivering on President Trump’s America First agenda including spearheading the administration’s crime crackdown including in DC and Memphis, record setting deregulation, unleashing American energy, dismantling drug cartels, and securing our border. In his new role, he will continue to deliver historic successes for the American people,” says White House spokesperson Lauren Bis.

WIRED spoke to Guerrero about where Miller goes from here in his new role, and the ways in which her understanding of Miller has evolved during Trump’s second term.

This conversation has been edited for length and clarity.

WIRED: Miller has now ascended to the most powerful role he has ever occupied. Is this something he has always been aiming for?

Jean Guerrero: Stephen Miller has had a disproportionate amount of power for a very long time. He's been so instrumental in so much of what Trump has done, starting from the first campaign to today. So regardless of the role that Stephen Miller's had, he always somehow turns that role into something more than it has been historically.

While I haven’t been necessarily talking to people in this administration, I suspected that this was coming, and when I heard the news I wasn't at all surprised. I remember thinking that Stephen Miller was going to, if not immediately then at some point, be named chief of staff because of the fact that he has been the most effective sycophant in Trump's orbit, and has been his longest-lasting and most trusted adviser.

Miller’s appointment happened only a few days after a midterm election that some called an “extinction-level” event for Republicans. Why right now?

I think Trump is hoping that Stephen Miller is going to somehow get him out of this mess. In the past, when he has been in trouble, he has turned to Stephen Miller and his expertise on anti-immigrant scapegoating to distract people and rally his base, and it has worked. But I suspect that this time it's not going to work, because the American people are so horrified by where Stephen Miller has already taken this war on immigrants.

I think many people on the right are realizing that the violence we saw in ICE’s conduct—particularly the shooting of Alex Pretti and Renee Good in Minneapolis earlier this year—is actually very unpopular. Do you think this will push Miller to change tack or to double down?

I see it as an impetus for him to double down, absolutely. He's always been driven by a desire to inflame and antagonize and horrify, from the time that he was a teenager to today. If there's any restraint at all, it'll be on the performative nature of the cruelty and maybe shifting towards a more performative nature on the war on the allies of immigrants and trying to characterize anyone who fights for social justice, not just for immigrants, as domestic terrorists. I think that is the war that Stephen Miller has been dying to fight for a very long time.

Stephen Miller was very influenced by the book The Camp of the Saints, the white supremacist, dystopian French novel. Reading that book was very illuminating for me, because the heroes are the characters who advocate for wiping out brown immigrants who are approaching by boat, who are cast as a threat to white civilization or Western civilization. In the book, the villains, in addition to the immigrants themselves, are the citizen allies of the immigrants.

You wrote your book during the first Trump administration. Has your understanding of Stephen Miller evolved since then?

My understanding of him has really changed. I used to see him fundamentally as a white nationalist, but now I see him more as a class supremacist and as a case study in oligarchic capture.

I came to this conclusion because when I first saw what the administration was doing on the foreign policy front, I was confused. Why would they be interfering in Venezuela or the elections in Honduras, when we know those are the exact types of actions that cause instability and migration? But when he told Jake Tapper, “You can talk all you want about international niceties and everything else. But we live in a world, in the real world, Jake, that is governed by strength, that is governed by force, that is governed by power,” I pulled out my book, and I read back through the sections where he's demanding that the custodial staff at his high school pick up his trash.

This is something he did in college too, where he would leave out his trash and protest any requests that he clean up after himself. When he was a teenager, he was really motivated by a desire to keep everybody in what he saw as their place. He didn't mind having immigrants around so long as they stayed “in their place,” where they were quietly serving him and picking up the trash that he was throwing on the floor or leaving on the cafeteria tables.

I realized that before Stephen Miller was a white nationalist, he was a class supremacist.

He hated poor people. He hated people who were less well-off than him, because he thought they were inferior to him.

Has this understanding changed the way you think about Miller’s focus on immigration now?

By focusing all the attention on the war on immigrants, it distracts us from understanding that when you have one sector of the population that is being criminalized and forced to stay in the shadows, then they cannot link arms with other workers to demand to live with dignity. Under this new administration, he has reverted to his most authentic self. Stephen Miller wants a servant class, not a working class.

#438. Skydance outlines its $6 billion in planned cost savings. Here's the breakdown

Skydance is pulling back the curtain on how it plans to achieve at least $6 billion in cost savings in the next three years.

"By combining two global streaming services, two film and TV studio groups, two television portfolios, and overlapping corporate functions, we can reduce duplicative spend across an expense base of more than $50 billion," the company wrote in a letter to shareholders Monday as part of Paramount Skydance's third-quarter earnings release.

Skydance, the company formed by the combination of Paramount Skydance and Warner Bros. Discovery, said it projects at least $2.2 billion in cost reductions from eliminating "redundant back-office and corporate functions," including eliminating management layers and ramping the use of artificial intelligence. Those savings also account for "rationalizing" its office footprint.

Skydance expects at least $1 billion in savings from streamlining marketing and promotional spending. And around $1.4 billion in savings will come from unifying its streaming businesses and broadcast architecture and integrating back-end technology.

A final $1.4 billion in savings will be tied to "optimizing third-party spend," eliminating overlapping expenses, and consolidating purchasing decisions to one centralized group, Skydance said.

"We are confident in achieving these savings, drawing on our playbook from Paramount," the company wrote. "That program is on track to achieve over $2.7 billion in run-rate efficiencies by the end of 2026."

Some of that cost-cutting has already begun.

Last month, Skydance began offering voluntary buyouts to employees ahead of official layoffs. At the same time, the company adopted a five-day-per-week in-office policy. Additionally, last week saw the departure of eight executives — four from Warner Bros. and four from Paramount.

The company has previously said it plans to eventually merge the Paramount+ and HBO Max streaming services into a single platform.

On Monday, Skydance said it would take the first step toward that goal early next year, offering a bundle of the streaming services "at an even greater value."

Co-CEO Ynon Kreiz told investors during the company's earnings call that Skydance is also looking for ways to optimize its content budget. The company spends more than $30 billion annually on shows and films.

"As one company, we can make smarter choices about how we distribute and monetize both new content and library content," Kreiz said.

Skydance noted that it's expecting a $200 million headwind in the fourth quarter from theatrical underperformances at Warner Bros. studio related to already launched titles and upcoming releases.

The company will also utilize AI to speed up production and decrease costs to make films, co-CEO and chairman David Ellison said on the call.

"We think these tools will help unlock more original storytelling by being able to make the budget more digestible to be able to take that amount of risk," Ellison said.

The updates came alongside third-quarter results for the legacy Paramount Skydance company that included revenue of $7.03 billion, which was roughly in line with StreetAccount estimates of $7.04 billion, and adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $1 billion, topping StreetAccount estimates of $913.5 million.

However, adjusted earnings per share of 45 cents were not immediately comparable to StreetAccount EPS estimates.

The results don't include the impact of the Warner Bros. Discovery acquisition, which closed after the end of the third quarter.

Skydance detailed business units of the combined entity, including studios, direct-to-consumer and TV, and said it will provide full-year guidance during its fourth-quarter earnings report early next year.

"Going forward, we will provide guidance on an annual basis, as we prioritize long-term value creation, which we believe will be more consistent with our strategic approach," the company told shareholders. "We will continue to provide quarterly updates, including certain forward-looking commentary where applicable, which will give investors visibility into our near-term progress of the business."

#434. How GM is trying to talk used car shoppers into buying new vehicles

Affordability challenges are pushing some buyers out of the new vehicle market. Many are choosing used cars instead.

In turn, automakers are looking for ways to compete with many of the online retailers grabbing market share in the used car market and trying to bring some of those customers back into a new vehicle.

New vehicle sales tend to be more profitable for automakers, but reselling trade-ins as used vehicles is highly profitable for dealers. Used car prices are relatively strong, but newer online retailers such as Carvana and CarMax have made the market more competitive for dealers and other incumbent used car sellers.

"The internet and technology and apps on our phone have changed every aspect of retail, and probably one of the slowest areas of retail to have adopted to online and digital shopping has been the auto industry," said Ivan Feinseth, partner, chief investment officer and director of research at Tigress Financial Partners.

General Motors is a key example of automakers' attempts to address this shifting market. The automaker has revamped its certified used vehicle program, which now operates under the CarBravo name. It's essentially an online portal through which Chevrolet, Buick and GMC dealers can list their certified used vehicles and make them available to customers all over the U.S.

About 1,615 of GM's 3,700 dealers are enrolled in the program, according to the company. The only GM brand not involved is Cadillac, which maintains its own program.

"Certified" used or "preowned" vehicles are used cars that have undergone a thorough inspection and reconditioning. They typically come with guarantees and warranties from the manufacturer.

Many automakers have certified used programs for their own cars. But GM, as part of its revamp, is using CarBravo to certify vehicles made by other automakers. A lot of those cars arrive at dealerships as trade-ins, and often dealers will sell them to wholesale auctions, said John Fitzpatrick, senior manager of the CarBravo program.

"Wholesaling doesn't bring nearly the revenue that selling it to a new customer does," he said.

GM has said it is planning to net about 250,000 sales in its CarBravo program in 2026. Fitzpatrick said he doesn't expect that even half of those will end up being new car sales.

But through CarBravo, the customer gets access to GM's dealership network for service, something Fitzpatrick says is a key differentiator between its program and growing online sellers. CarBravo vehicles come with various warranties, depending on the age and mileage on the car.

GM said the program is driving more traffic into local dealerships, where purchases are typically finalized. That creates an opportunity to convert a used car shopper into a new car buyer. So far, the automaker said about 38% of the shoppers initially looking for used cars end up buying new.

A customer who wants to buy a full-size truck can look on the website and compare used Chevrolet Silverados alongside used Ford F-150s and Ram 1500s, for example. The buyer might settle on a used truck from any of those brands, but a conversation with a dealer can push them in a different direction.

"The customer picks up the phone, but in the back of their mind, they maybe always have been a traditional, more new car customer," Fitzpatrick said.

"You've shown the customer, you're going to pay $500 [per month] for this used F-150, but you may pay $510 or $515 for this new Chevy," he said. "And now all of a sudden the customer says, yeah, maybe I can do that because I'd rather have that new vehicle than the used truck."

While the average transaction price of a new vehicle is about $50,000, the average 3-year-old used car costs about $30,000 as of September, according to Cox Automotive.

However, industry analysts say customers base their purchasing decisions on the cost of the monthly payment more than on the sticker price of the car.

New cars often come with longer-term loan options than used ones, Feinseth said, which can result in lower monthly payments.

"On a 1- or 2-year-old car, you may be only stuck with a 3- or 4-year loan," he said. "On a new car, you can get a 5-year, 6-year loan."

The share of new car loans lasting 84 months hit a new record in the third quarter, at more than 1 in 4 loans, according to Edmunds.

Meanwhile, used car prices in some affordable segments have risen, according to Cox Automotive, and about three quarters of consumers express some interest in certified cars.

But the key is building the relationship with the customer, he said. About 50% of the buyers GM has gained through the program appear to be new to the brand, meaning there is no record of them in any of the automaker's customer databases. About 70% had never visited the dealership they ended up purchasing from.

"The goal is really to get that customer engaged with the store, give them the option of building a relationship with the dealer," he said. "The dealer can really help the customer figure out what works for their budget."

It's a boon for dealers too: Customers buying warranty-backed used cars often return to the dealership for service visits — which often account for about half of a dealership's profits. GM can also sell subscriptions to connected vehicle services, such as its OnStar, which provides safety, security and navigation services.

"That is a big part of General Motors' business going forward, is to sell the software and subscription services like OnStar and eventually to the cars that are enabled with Super Cruise," GM's advanced driver-assistance system, Feinseth said.

GM isn't the only automaker trying to use its certified preowned vehicle program to lure buyers.

Detroit rival Ford Motor also certifies vehicles made by other manufacturers through its Ford Blue Advantage program. It is on track to do 450,000 total sales in 2026, more than GM's 250,000 target. It didn't specify how many of those buyers end up purchasing new vehicles.

Toyota told CNBC in an email that about 40% of its used car shoppers end up buying new instead.

Honda didn't give specifics about its program. But Lance Woelfer, American Honda's vice president of automobile sales, said conversions happen "probably every day at dealerships throughout the country."

"But it also is more about that first interaction with the consumer," Woelfer said. "And there are people in any market that prefer to buy a used vehicle, but appreciate the confidence that a [certified preowned designation] can give them."

Woelfer said those customers come back to get their vehicles serviced at a rate about 18% higher than someone who buys a non-certified used car. They also return to purchase a new car at a rate about 7% to 8% higher in the future, he said.

"It's about that first relationship that allows us to then have that customer loyalty for life," he added. "And that's where our focus is. We've been doing it a long time, and we've seen a lot of benefits from the certified business."
