#433. Even Black Friday Will Be More Expensive Than Usual

Black Friday, the deal-drenched marketing blitz that kicks off the holiday shopping spree, might feel more muted this year.

That’s because just about everything is getting more expensive. As the global economy reckons with challenges from the AI industry’s memory crisis and the ongoing war in Iran, prices for many products have risen, especially consumer electronics like smartphones and gaming consoles.

While many sales have already begun, the prices on offer may feel less like a deal than in years past. Weaker deals will likely revolve around products that use a lot of memory (RAM). Gadgets like robot vacuums, headphones, and other products that don’t rely as much on memory may see better prices.

“Things are getting more expensive, and they'll probably continue to get more expensive well into 2027, maybe even 2028,” says Jitesh Ubrani, director of Consumer Devices Research at IDC. “When it comes to PCs, tablets, those kinds of devices, that's where everyone's going to struggle.”

Case in point: Amazon, the absolute behemoth of Black Friday and Cyber Monday sales, has hiked the prices of its latest Kindles, tablets, and Echo speakers, meaning its next sale on those devices likely won’t impress compared to years past.

How to Beat Surveillance Pricing Before It Bleeds You Dry

Buying a Used iPhone Makes More Sense Than Ever

The WIRED Gear Team’s Tips on Ways to Save Money

Airfare Keeps Going Up. Here Are Some Tricks to Find Cheap(er) Tickets

7 Apps to Help You Trim Down Subscriptions—and Save Money

Black Friday and Cyber Monday remain the best shopping days of the year, says Edgar Dworsky, a consumer advocate who has long used his website Consumer World to call out companies that stealthily raise product prices.

“However, just because an item is advertised with significant savings claimed, doesn't mean it is a good deal or the best price,” Dworsky wrote in a message to WIRED.

Dworsky recommends tracking Amazon’s prices by monitoring price-history websites like CamelCamelCamel. Google has a built-in price-tracking tool in the Chrome browser, but many other third-party extensions do the job as well.

“Because of high memory prices, laptops, desktops, and SSDs will still be on sale,” Dworsky predicts. “But not nearly as low-priced as previously offered.”

It might be a good move to buy a used or refurbished gadget. Sean Cleland, vice president of Mobility Tech at the product resale company BStock Solutions, says some manufacturers are leaning into selling refurbished units more than ever, even buying them from resellers like BStock. They're filling gaps in the budget phone market by offering up refurbished products.

“The new trend that we're seeing is the [manufacturers] buying back their own stuff in the secondary market so that they can refurbish it and sell it as authorized refurbished or certified renewed,” Cleland says. “They're trying to get anything and everything back.”

What Black Friday will be good for, it seems, is the companies selling all this stuff. They get to set the costs, after all. With all the economic turmoil in the market, companies have been adept at passing additional costs to consumers.

“There's no good news right now for consumers,” Ubrani says. “It's unfortunate that the consumers are the ones who are going to struggle because of all this.”

So maybe the deals won’t be great. But they might be the best you can get for a while. The memory crisis isn’t ending anytime soon, so now might be the time to get something you need. If you do that, Ubrani recommends making sure it’s something you actually need. And something that will keep working for a long time.

“They should be purchasing the most amount of performance that they can afford today,” Ubrani says. “We're going to face elevated pricing for years. You want that device to last you for a while.”

#431. Microsoft apologizes for global outage: We regret the impact, can rule out cyberattack

Updated on Friday, Nov. 13 at 12:35 p.m. ET — Microsoft has now issued a public apology; our original story has been updated with the latest statement from the company.

On Friday morning, as Microsoft worked to bring an end to a global Outlook and Microsoft 365 outage, the company issued a public apology to its customers.

In a statement posted to its X account, the company wrote, "We are deeply sorry for the disruption this incident has caused our customers and partners. We know the trust they place in our services and take that responsibility extremely seriously."

The company also ruled out a cyberattack or problems related to AI agents, and pledged to "provide a full post-incident report once our review is complete."

The Microsoft outage started on Thursday, Nov. 13 and continued into Friday morning, disrupting work for people around the globe. As Microsoft 365 services such as Outlook, Teams, and Copilot started to come back online Friday, the company initially issued a statement expressing its "regret" to customers.

"We’re seeing encouraging recovery progress, with service availability and email delivery performance improving across a growing portion of affected infrastructure," said Charles Lamanna, EVP, Copilot, Agents and Platform at Microsoft, in a statement to Mashable. "We know our customers rely on our services to get important work done every day, and regret the impact these outages had on those who depend on us. Our teams are reviewing what happened and how we can prevent similar issues going forward." #429. Microsoft outage: Does Microsoft owe you money? What to know.

Microsoft's massive, productivity-destroying software outage appears to be receding as of Friday morning, but that's only the start of the process for businesses looking to get some of their Office 365 money back.

Several important enterprise software products, such as Outlook and Teams, were functionally offline for many users on Thursday and into Friday. Since Office 365 is a service businesses have to pay for, it raises the question: Can those businesses receive some form of compensation from Microsoft for the time lost?

The answer to that question is nuanced. Let's get into it.

According to helpful resources like these explainers from Quartz and Office Watch, the short answer is no; Microsoft does not owe anyone money as a result of this outage. But that doesn't mean the company doesn't owe any compensation, as it offers credits (essentially a reduction on the next bill) in the event of an outage.

One important thing to note: You have to request a credit. Microsoft doesn't offer them automatically.

Purchasing Microsoft's software suite involves a service-level agreement (SLA). This SLA includes provisions related to outages and compensation. In this case, Microsoft's SLA promises 99.9 percent uptime each month for each piece of software in the suite, such as OneDrive and Outlook. If any of those services fall below the 99.9 percent threshold, it's possible to claim a credit. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

"Possible" is the key word here, though, as both Quartz and Office Watch make it very clear that Microsoft is extremely strict about how it calculates downtime in these instances. The actual math is too complicated to fully get into here, but Microsoft counts downtime in terms of "user minutes," or the amount of time the services were down multiplied by the number of users demonstrably affected.

Credits are also prorated depending on how much uptime was lost, so a small amount of downtime might only be worth a 25 or 50 percent credit. Where this gets even more complicated is that Microsoft calculates credits according to each individual service, not the entire software bundle. If you only lost service time in Outlook, but other parts of the Office 365 suite worked, you'd only get a small credit for Outlook.

All of this is to say that, depending on the size of the affected business and the amount of downtime it experienced, one of these credits could be worth only a few measly dollars.

To be very clear, individual workers who lost service time due to the big outage cannot file a claim or receive compensation. Businesses who license this software from Microsoft can do it, but even that is more complicated than you'd think.

Businesses only have until the end of December to file these claims, for starters. If a business purchased Microsoft software directly from Microsoft, it can go to the Office admin center to file a claim. They will need to provide a lot of information, such as incident IDs and specific descriptions of how long service was unavailable to users. From there, Microsoft will perform its own calculations to determine whether the business receives a credit.

Many businesses do not purchase these Microsoft products directly from the company, instead obtaining them through Cloud Solution Provider (CSP) partners or other indirect resellers. In that case, the business will have to bug the provider to submit a claim on their behalf. Depending on the provider, that may or may not happen in a timely or productive manner.

Given the number of hoops that businesses have to jump through to get one of these credits, one could argue it's not worth the hassle, depending on the size of a business and how much time it lost.

Alex has spent most of the last decade reviewing games, smartphones, headphones, and laptops, and he doesn’t plan on stopping anytime soon. He is also a Pisces, a cat lover, and a Kansas City sports fan. Alex can be found on Bluesky at yelix.bsky.social.

#428. Rivian CEO says new R2 SUV, autonomy make 2027 an ‘inflection year’ as EV maker marks five years since IPO

It's been five years since Rivian Automotive celebrated its initial public offering with CEO and founder RJ Scaringe ringing the Nasdaq opening bell from the company's factory in Normal, Illinois, while the company's sleek vehicles sat outside the stock exchange in Times Square.

It was a euphoric time for all-electric vehicle companies that has since diminished. And much like the EV hype, Rivian's stock price is down more than 80% since the Amazon-backed company debuted on the Nasdaq at $78 per share on Nov. 10, 2021. That includes a nearly 30% decline this year.

A host of internal and external factors have contributed to Rivian's stock decline, but the company continues to be the most promising EV maker to emerge during the early 2020s, as most of its peer group to go public during that time no longer exists.

Rivian founder and CEO RJ Scaringe acknowledges that not everything has gone to plan amid supply chain shortages, geopolitical tensions and slower-than-expected consumer EV adoption, but he said he believes the next year will prove to be a catalyst for the company and investors.

"2027 is really an inflection year for us," Scaringe told CNBC during an exclusive interview. "I'm excited to start to really show what we believe so fully, the potential of the business."

Scaringe's bullishness comes as Rivian ramps up production of its midsize R2 SUV — meant to expand the brand to the mainstream market — and as the company continues to invest in its next generation of vehicle software and autonomy.

Scaringe said the R2, with an entry-level model at about $45,000, remains the core business to be able to showcase its newest technologies. Rivian delivered a record 19,248 vehicles in the third quarter of 2026, up 46% year over year, and said R2 was already its best-selling vehicle by volume, outselling both its R1 flagship products and its commercial van.

"That's going to define the business. The path to profitability, the path to very significant growth in revenue is all built around R2, and then some of the variants that come following R2," Scaringe said.

For the most recent period, Rivian said the automotive cost of goods sold per vehicle improved by more than $12,000 quarter over quarter, helped by a higher R2 mix and better fixed-cost leverage.

Rivian also is in the midst of building a new multibillion-dollar Georgia factory that Scaringe has said is necessary to get the company to the volumes it needs to achieve net profitability.

Its sole factory in Normal, Illinois, is capable of producing 215,000 vehicles annually. That includes up to 155,000 R2s alongside R1 models and electric vans. Rivian said the Georgia plant will be capable of producing up to 300,000 units annually once the initial phase construction is completed in late 2028.

Of course, all of that means additional capital spending and pressure on the company to improve profits.

"We've made the decision to simultaneously work towards profitability … and deeply invest in our technology, and in particular in autonomy," Scaringe said.

Rivian's operating performance is improving, but several Wall Street analysts expect the company's investment requirements to rise significantly in 2027.

Rivian generated $176 million of consolidated gross profit in the third quarter, but its automotive business posted an $80 million gross loss, while its software and services unit generated $256 million of gross profit at a 42% margin.

Wall Street analysts broadly agree Rivian's R2 launch is showing encouraging early demand and lowering manufacturing costs, but they're concerned about whether that can offset rising autonomy spending, the Georgia factory buildout and continued cash burn.

"Funding has long been a key debate for Rivian investors," Deutsche Bank analyst Edison Yu said in an Oct. 30 investor note after the company reported quarterly results that exceeded Wall Street's expectations. "The notion of a multi-year (even if credible) path to cash flow breakeven will be less attractive to investors."

Rivian said during its late October earnings call that its total available future liquidity is expected to be about $13 billion when factoring in available credit and money from partnerships with Volkswagen and Uber as well as a $4.5 billion U.S. Department of Energy loan for the Georgia plant.

Rivian said its cash, cash equivalents and short-term investments balance was $5.5 billion to end the third quarter.

The company may still need to raise more capital. Scaringe said Rivian plans to be "opportunistic" about any additional capital raises, which have previously included equity deals with Volkswagen and Uber.

"As we think about the environment looking out, we want to continue to position ourselves to be in a place to invest in the technology. And so that means we'll be opportunistic as it pertains to capital. But I want to be clear: We're like very focused on getting the profitability," Scaringe said.

The company has significantly upgraded the software capabilities and hardware on its vehicles to prepare them for autonomy technologies, or physical artificial intelligence, as Scaringe calls it.

The next step in that is releasing "point-to-point" driving capabilities, in which the vehicles can drive on their own from destination to destination with the driver acting as a monitor in case of any unexpected problems.

U.S. EV leader Tesla is the only automaker to currently offer those features on its vehicles. Scaringe said Rivian plans to not only catch up to Tesla's capabilities but exceed them as it uses multiple sensor types to allow the vehicle to "see" better, especially in inclement weather.

"That's not like the finish line. ... Think of it as like the starting point where you suddenly have a more apples to apples comparison between Rivian and Tesla," Scaringe said.

The release of point-to-point over the coming weeks and months, first to employees and then to customers, is expected to be followed by autonomous vehicles with Uber by 2028.

Scaringe said he believes such technologies will become a differentiator for the company in the coming years.

"I think it will go from a purchase consideration for maybe 10% of the buying public that's in the top five things considered in making a purchase decision to the No. 1 or No. 2 purchase consideration for the vast majority of buyers," Scaringe said.

Rivian plans to offer more details about its AI and autonomous plans during a second annual investor technology event on Dec. 9.

"We view this as a likely positive catalyst as the event could set a path for AV features driving incremental vehicle demand & higher [software] revenue in 2027, building on 2026 growth," TD Cowen analyst Itay Michaeli said in an investor note last week.

Rivian's stock is currently rated overweight with a $17.70 target price, according to analyst estimates compiled by FactSet. That would be a roughly 26% increase from its Monday closing price but still a far cry from its IPO.

#427. This Startup Is Betting on AI Microdramas to Get More Black Stories in Hollywood

A zombie virus has overtaken South Los Angeles. Authorities, hoping to contain the outbreak, have decided to barricade the neighborhood, leaving residents to make do on their own. So begins They Left Us to Die, a new microdrama from media company Mansa that runs 30 episodes and is entirely AI-generated.

“The plague was made here, made on us, on purpose,” says Imani Cross, an emergency room nurse desperate for a cure to help her brother DJ, who’s been infected.

On Instagram, the trailer drew mostly favorable reaction when it was released on October 29. A cascade of fire emoji filled the comments section as anticipation mounted. But the following day, as the first episode was set to go live at midnight, new tensions began to stir. Why had Mansa created a show with zero human roles? Weren’t actors David Oyelowo and Nate Parker, the company’s founders, about uplifting artists of color, not erasing them?

“Mansa was one of the first vertical apps that was supposed to support and cultivate black talent, but here they are … another AI sellout,” said one user on Threads. “Why would any self-respecting casting director, producer, or director ever consider hiring Oleyowo again after this?” posted another. Actress Yvette Nicole Brown responded with a single note of disapproval: “*sigh* Mmkay,” she wrote, adding a gif of Viola Davis expressing irritation.

Parker wasn’t surprised by the backlash. “When we hear the words AI, it immediately drives us to the realities of what we are facing and the real lives it’s impacting,” Parker tells WIRED.

The problem is that while Hollywood is desperate for more expansive Black stories, not everyone agrees on the best way to make them happen.

As part of Mansa’s development strategy, the company says it is using AI as a chess piece rather than a replacement for human-centered content. The power comes in leveraging a show like They Left Us to Die as a way to bring more awareness to what Black audiences will support. He describes the approach as “holding the line on our very existence” in an industry that’s consolidating, and being able to “use all the tools that we have at our disposal to make sure we can staff up around shows that get greenlit.”

In the shrinking Hollywood of today, a series about a Black ER nurse fighting off a zombie virus unleashed on her neighborhood by white people would likely struggle to find the backing of a major studio without first having proved its value 10 times over. Mansa’s strategy is to test the appetite of audiences with its AI shows. Parker believes this will demonstrate to studios that there is a demand for those kinds of stories before committing to a live production.

Parker says studio heads are even more reluctant in this climate. But with microdramas, “you get to test a piece of content, not just test it in a way of proof of concept like a short film, but you get to monetize it,” he says. “You get to track data around engagement and see if this is something the audience wants.”

As attention spans shrink, microdramas have sprung up as a preferred portal of entertainment for both studios and young people. They’re vertically shot, minute-long soaps that are produced at a fraction of the cost of a TV show and mirror back the kind of frenzied consumption that has come to define modern entertainment. As streamers like ReelShort and DramaBox dominate the industry, stories anchored around romance, billionaire fantasies, revenge, and supernatural sagas have become the genres driving a boom largely fueled by women viewers.

An Omdia analysis of mobile usage in the fourth quarter of 2025 found that users spent an average of 10 more minutes per day on ReelShort (36 minutes) compared to Prime Video (26 minutes) and Netflix (20 minutes), with the firm predicting that the microdrama market will reach $14 billion in 2027.

Hoorae Media, Issa Rae’s production company, expanded into the sector this year with two microdramas, including Screen Time, a couples thriller comedy that, as of October, had over 580 million views across TikTok and its in-house microdrama app, PineDrama. There are also upstarts Juccee Shorts and Urban Operas, which is launching with six series later this month, that are angling to offer more diverse stories for audiences.

Among Gen Z, watching short-form video is now more common than watching movies, accounting for 26 percent of weekly media time among young adults aged 18 to 24, according to a recent survey by the entertainment analytics firm Luminate.

Streamers are also investing more into the format and have even started to redesign their own user experience to keep pace with Gen Z’s twitchy consumption habits. Between April and August, Peacock, HBO Max, Prime Video, Paramount+, Netflix, and Disney+ all launched a short-form vertical feed on their respective mobile app.

Maria Rua Aguete, global head of media and entertainment at Omdia, suspects that AI will change the economics of the industry. But more cheaply produced content doesn’t automatically mean more viewers. “Ultimately, audiences will decide what succeeds,” she says, adding that there is a “significant opportunity” to tap into overlooked demos.

The backlash in response to They Left Us to Die is a direct symptom of this rapid evolution. “What Nate is trying to do, no matter how practical and logical it is, he’s not reading the room and understanding that people don’t care about that. They just see technology as stealing their opportunities,” says J. Christopher Hamilton, an associate professor of communications at Syracuse and entertainment lawyer.

The reality of Hollywood is dire. The $110 billion acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery by Paramount Skydance is projected to eliminate up to 4,500 film and television jobs in Los Angeles, according to the county’s Department of Economic Opportunity and Film Office. That reality can feel doubly crushing for Black artists and industry workers because they’re already vastly underrepresented.

The concern in response to They Left Us to Die highlighted the desperation that so many people are feeling around losing opportunities they were already struggling to land. Despite Black, Indigenous, and people of color moviegoers over-indexing as ticket buyers, representing the majority of opening weekend domestic ticket sales, Black actors accounted for only 6.5 percent of movie leads while Black directors made up 3.3 percent of releases in 2025, according to the 2026 UCLA Hollywood Diversity report.

As a distributor “showcasing diaspora-focused content,” Parker wants to prove that there is value in being able to go from an idea to a script to market in a matter of weeks rather than the years it might take through the Hollywood system. Mansa has produced nine live-action microdramas so far, in addition to two that are fully AI-generated. The company declined to share data on how its AI series were performing.

But what Mansa is attempting is also about trust for viewers, some of whom wonder whether Parker is worthy of it. In 1999, Parker was charged and later acquitted of sexual assault while in college. The case resurfaced following the release of The Birth of a Nation, his 2016 film about a slave rebellion led by Nat Turner; it depicted a fictionalized rape scene as motivation for the uprising. “Nate Parker been a pariah,” one person wrote on Threads under the announcement about Mansa’s AI series, as more people poured on.

Whether Mansa succeeds or fails, Hamilton believes AI microdramas are “too compelling a business proposition” for the industry to ignore.

“Real-life actors and storytellers aren’t going away completely, but I do think we’re going to see a resetting of the table, and some people are in denial about that,” Hamilton says. “When it hits, it’s going to be like a blackout. No one’s going to know which way is up.”

Updated on Friday, Nov. 13 at 2:11 p.m. ET — Microsoft has reported "broad recovery and stability" across its services and published an apology on X. We've updated this story to reflect the latest updates from the company.

Updated on Friday, Nov. 13 at 1:08 a.m. ET — Microsoft is still looking into the cause of the outage, but states that at present it is "not aware of any malicious activity that is causing this incident."

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 10:58 p.m. ET — Unfortunately, Microsoft's efforts to bring servers back online aren't going as planned. "We've monitored the status of the changes applied to improve serviceability and confirmed they're not taking as expected," Microsoft wrote in an update on X.

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 8:07 p.m. ET — Microsoft has revised its list of features and services impacted by this outage to include Microsoft Outlook, SharePoint Online, Teams, Copilot AI, and Microsoft 365, though Azure appears to be operational.

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 7:53 p.m. ET — Microsoft has issued a new update stating that the servers they've brought back online may become unresponsive again when they receive traffic. The company is currently investigating the issue and limiting the types of traffic going to these servers in some locations.

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 6:23 p.m. ET — Microsoft states that it is bringing some servers back online, and "they remain stable so far." Users should expect a "gradual recovery."

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 5:26 p.m. ET — Microsoft services remained down on Thursday evening, as Microsoft acknowledged ongoing problems in an update posted to X.

If Outlook wouldn't load for you on Thursday (or on Friday morning), Microsoft now has a theory about what went wrong.

In its initial update about the outage, Microsoft stated that Microsoft Exchange Online was at the root of the problem. Many users were unable to access Microsoft 365 services related to Exchange Online, including Outlook, Teams, SharePoint Online, Copilot, Defender XDR, and more. Exchange Online is the back-end, cloud-based tool that provides data updates for front-end, user-facing tools like Microsoft Outlook.

In updates posted to X and on its Service Health Status Page, Microsoft stated that some of its servers became "unexpectedly unavailable" on Thursday. With parts of its infrastructure under a heavier-than-usual load across multiple regions, the company said the extra strain was likely contributing to connection problems.

The company also stated in an X post that the failures may have been caused by a "specific component that's responsible for processing server health," and that servers were going offline after failing automated health checks.

When Microsoft found that the initial fixes weren't having the desired effects, the company also tried rolling back recent changes to its servers, while continuing to balance traffic across its network. The company is also investigating why the health checks failed in the first place.

At first, Microsoft stated that it hadn't found a recent change that coincided with the start of the outage.

However, in a Friday morning statement posted to X, the company attributed the outage to a recent network update. "The incident was introduced by an issue during an earlier network capacity upgrade that surfaced following a recent network change. We have addressed the underlying issue and continue to restore service to remaining customers and regions."

While Microsoft has now provided a partial explanation of what caused the global Microsoft outage, the picture remains incomplete. #422. Uber confirms thousands of driver IDs compromised in data breach

Fireworks AI explained that unnamed cybercriminals, allegedly motivated by a desire to steal and then sell LLM tokens, breached its cloud provider to access its development system. #421. TCL QM7L Mini-LED TV Review (2026)

Are we entering the era of TV “dupes”? Because to the untrained eye, or even a reasonably trained one, it’s hard to distinguish TCL’s mini-LED-powered QM7L from a lower-end OLED. So when your brother-in-law plops down in front of your new TV to watch the Lions and says, “Wow, you splurged, eh?” you can just nod and give a wink. Dupe accomplished.

I know this because the QM7L arrived close on the heels of a new budget OLED, the Roku Pro Series, so I was able to compare the two side by side. Results: surprising. Although it has a potentially problematic design element and an AI that’s not exactly Claude-level sophisticated, the QM7L might just be the best midrange TV you can buy right now.

Available in sizes ranging from 55 inches (which I tested) to a mammoth 98 inches, the QM7L boasts a virtually borderless screen that stands on a pedestal instead of the usual feet. That’s a win if you own a small piece of furniture that might not accommodate the width of such feet, and I’d argue it’s more aesthetically pleasing too.

However, it does pose a problem: Because the pedestal base sticks out in front by a little over 4 inches, it doesn’t allow a soundbar to sit snug up to the screen. You could balance it on top of the base, but that looks awkward. And because it slopes down, the soundbar doesn’t even sit level.

What’s more, although the base has two height options, the taller one leaves the bottom of the TV just 2.25 inches off your TV stand. That may be enough clearance for ultra-compact soundbars like the Bose TV Speaker, but something like the Sonos Arc, which measures 3 inches high, ends up blocking a sliver of the screen.

The pedestal is also kind of a pain to assemble, with little help from the tiny diagrams in TCL’s otherwise decent setup guide. There are eight small screws used to connect the riser to the base, four more to attach it to the TV, and a bit of confusion over which of 10 mounting holes you’re meant to use.

These are hardly dealbreakers. If you’re planning to wall-mount the TV, the base becomes a non-issue. Same if you’re skipping a soundbar altogether. Or maybe you’re willing to live with it sitting out in front a few inches.

Setting up the TV itself is largely a breeze, especially if you already have the Google Home app on your phone. Even though I’m an iPhone guy, I was able to scan an onscreen QR code and use the app to move quickly through the setup process. An account sign-in and Wi-Fi network selection later, I just had to wait on a software update before heading to the couch.

TCL’s unremarkable remote has sharp edges, the usual mishmash of Google TV buttons, and a pretty disorganized layout overall. On the plus side, it’s backlit and comes with six app-shortcut buttons.

One head-scratcher: The penultimate TV-setup screen notes that I can “customize the star button on the remote,” using it as a shortcut for this or that, which sounds great except that there is no star button.

Speaking of hallucinations (kidding!), Google Gemini comes baked into the Google TV UI, which is reasonably easy to navigate but not without a few annoyances. For example, the top menu includes a Live TV page—home to various FAST channels, simple enough—but also a TCL page that contains … other FAST channels.

As for Gemini, it’s nice to have but hardly essential. Just talk into the remote and you can ask for, say, movies about sharks, or learn who invented the Steadicam. The voice recognition is responsive, and the responses arrive quickly. Too bad it’s not hands-free; you have to press and hold a button on the remote to use it. Gemini also dishes up “watch now” options, and you can use it to create custom images and video clips, if you want. Unfortunately, while Gemini can accommodate basic TV commands (“Start Netflix,” “Mute the sound,” etc.), it can’t do things like open picture settings or toggling off motion-smoothing—a bummer because those commands would actually save you a little time. Instead, such requests turn into basic web searches, returning only information on how to access those settings yourself.

I was a bit disappointed with Google TV’s screensaver. It lets you draw from various sources, including an art gallery and your own photo library, but it doesn’t offer any “art mode” options like a mat overlay or automated brightness reduction. You also can’t adjust the amount of idle time before the screensaver kicks on. An art TV this is not.

Software quibbles notwithstanding, the QM7L is a joy to behold. Whatever you care to make of TCL's marketing lingo (SQD Mini-LED, the “S” stands for “Super”), the results can’t be denied: dazzling color and ocean-floor blacks that really do bring OLED to mind. In fact, I’d argue that this TV “pops” more than the aforementioned Roku OLED—not because the colors are more vibrant, but because there’s greater brightness behind them.

While watching Pixar’s Coco, for example, both TVs produced glorious hues, but my eye was more drawn to the TCL because the reds, greens, and blues just seemed to burst a bit more. (OLEDs, especially on the lower end, do tend to struggle with brightness, as detailed in my Roku Pro Series review.)

Even watching something like East of Eden on Netflix, with its decidedly muted color palette, it was the brightness that stood out; the QM7L revealed a bit more detail inside the Trask house. Interestingly, I also spotted a difference in the Netflix logo: On the TCL, it blazed a fire-engine red; the Roku version looked more subtle, almost orange in comparison.

Make no mistake, both TVs impressed me greatly. But seeing the two alongside each other, I liked the QM7L’s picture just a little better. I also watched sports and played Xbox games—both well served by the panel’s native 144-Hz refresh rate—and marveled at the smooth action and sharp lines.

Not everything was perfect, however. There were a few cases where contrast and brightness seemed a little lacking, like during an episode of the criminally underrated Halt and Catch Fire on the Roku Channel. Checking the settings, I found that the brightness had been dialed down quite a bit—but not by me.

The prime suspect? TCL’s AI-powered “smart picture optimization,” a common bit of marketing in modern TVs. If there was any behind-the-scenes intelligence at work here, I struggled to find it. Indeed, I had to manually adjust the brightness for that show.

Even more frustrating, where was motion-smoothing management? This scourge of modern television should be off by default. With the QM7L, it arrives in full ruinous glory—and the AI isn’t smart enough to disable it in situations where it doesn’t belong: namely, everything except sports and gaming. Luckily, it’s not buried 10 menus deep; you can toggle it off pretty easily. But this is one area where Roku proved superior, its Smart Picture Max adeptly adjusting motion-smoothing for most viewing.

As for sound, the QM7L is the rare TV that doesn’t straight-up suck, thanks to tiny, down-facing speakers buried inside the cabinet. This one has two Bang & Olufsen–branded cones you can actually see, and because they’re embedded in the backside, they face backward as well. That’s not ideal for a wall-mount situation, as the audio would be forced to kind of squirt out from the sides, but the speakers do sound pretty good when the TV is freestanding. TCL doesn’t specify speaker wattage, but I can attest that they get plenty loud, and do so without distortion. However, I don’t understand why they’re positioned just a few inches apart, as the cabinet appears to have room for much more separation. Consequently, the soundstage feels pretty narrow; there’s very little “surround” to be found here. Even with this above-average audio, you’re still going to want a soundbar—hence my complaints about the pedestal stand earlier.

But if my main complaints are a wide pedestal stand and so-so AI integrations, that's good news for the QM7L. The most important thing, picture quality, is stunning for the price. You buy a TV for your eyes, and with this one, they're in for a treat.

#418. Fossil CEO says turnaround is ahead of plan as sales return to growth

Fossil Group posted quarterly results on Tuesday that could point to early signs of a turnaround for the legacy watchmaker.

The company, which has struggled to compete with new gadgets in the years since its sales and stock price peaked in the 2010s, has been quietly undertaking a comeback strategy under CEO Franco Fogliato. The company's fiscal third-quarter earnings indicate the strategy is beginning to bear fruit.

The company said Tuesday that it returned to profitability in the third quarter, posting operating income of $25.3 million, compared with an operating loss of $21.7 million last year. It also returned to sales growth, with net sales up 0.4% to $271.2 million. The company last reported a year-over-year sales increase in the first fiscal quarter of 2022.

"We're excited about improving dramatically the profitability of the company through a very strong and disciplined management of full-price sales as well as cost management," Fogliato told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

For the quarter ended Oct. 3, Fossil reported net income of $11.7 million, or 19 cents per share, compared with a net loss of $39.9 million, or 76 cents per share, the year prior. The company also said its operating expenses fell 11.1% to $137 million. The company last posted quarterly net income in the third fiscal quarter of 2022.

With the signs of growth, the retailer also raised its full-year outlook, guiding for full-year net sales of between $980 million and $990 million, which would mark a slight decline from just over $1 billion in net sales in the prior year. That compares with a previous expectation of net sales falling between 3% and 5%. Fossil also said it's expecting positive free cash flow by the end of the year.

Fogliato said Fossil had previously not expected sales growth for another year and a half.

The stronger quarter comes after the company saw slumping sales and financial losses over the past few years. It struggled with the rise of smartwatches, mounting debt and the decline in mall foot traffic.

Fossil stock has lost the vast majority of its value since the early-to-mid 2010s, and despite a recent surge, was trading under $8 a share on Tuesday.

To rightsize the business, Fogliato, who joined Fossil in 2024, said he focused on closing stores, moving away from promotions to full-price sales, improving marketing and broadening Fossil's product range. That meant shrinking the business to a sustainable baseline before trying to grow it.

The company saw a 5.9% decrease in direct-to-consumer sales for its third quarter, which Fogliato attributed to the company's "aggressive" downsizing.

"We simplified a lot of the business doing this," Fogliato said, adding that customers responded well when the company moved away from markdowns. "People tell me they are excited about what they are seeing in the marketplace now."

Fossil added that its key growth engine for the quarter was Asia, where net sales grew 15.1%. Fogliato said much of that growth was led by the India market.

At the same time, growing internationally is only part of the watchmaker's strategy as it tries to improve its performance in its home country.

"Priority number one is to grow our home market, and we're doing that," he said. "As retail is settling into a smaller, more profitable base, ready to grow again, we're entering 2027 with plenty of opportunities."

Fogliato said one key to growth was not trying to keep up with the smartwatch trend. While trying to make gains in the smartwatch space initially hampered Fossil's performance, it's now investing in analog watches again.

Smartwatch sales came in under $1 million for the quarter, but sales for traditional watches — which make up around 86% of Fossil's sales — grew 6.4%.

"Fossil is literally having a comeback, a strong comeback, a momentum, and we can see this," Fogliato said. "And it comes not only through the numbers — it comes with the excitement we see in the stores, and we think we're just at the beginning. We have so much work to do."

#416. Measles is surging in the U.S. — and outbreaks can cost millions

Measles outbreaks aren't just a public health threat. They can also be a financial burden on the U.S. — and the cost could only deepen as the once-eliminated disease spreads more widely.

The U.S. is expected to lose its official measles elimination status following a review by the Pan American Health Organization this week, after cases ratcheted to recent highs this year.

More than 5,100 measles cases have been reported nationwide in 2026 as of early November, marking the country's worst outbreak in more than three decades. Eight deaths associated with the disease have been confirmed by states so far in 2026: six in Pennsylvania, one in New York and one in Wisconsin.

Based on this year's case count, a new tool developed by researchers at Johns Hopkins University estimates an economic cost of nearly $83 million for the outbreaks. That total includes $56 million in public health response costs, almost $25 million in lost productivity and more than $1.8 million in direct medical expenses.

The tool also estimates that 2025 measles outbreaks in the U.S. — which involved 2,289 confirmed cases nationwide – carried an economic cost of more than $37 million.

The costs extend beyond treating people who become sick, accounting for the resources health departments spend investigating and containing outbreaks, as well as wages lost when people have to miss work. The tool uses more recent, state-specific cost and financing data than an initial study by the researchers, which relied on historical outbreak costs.

The estimates from the tool are not a definitive accounting of what the outbreaks actually cost. But it illustrates the hefty bill tied to the spread of a disease that can be prevented through vaccination, and the potential economic consequences of falling childhood immunization rates. CNBC previously reported on how the return of measles in the U.S. exposes weaknesses in the nation's public health system that raise concerns about the return of other eliminated diseases.

"We're starting to see a whole bunch of people who decided not to get vaccinated as kids or have never gotten exposed to measles because they benefited from herd immunity, who now make it into adulthood and risk potentially getting quite seriously ill and requiring very significant medical expenses, or dying at the hospital," Bryan Patenaude, a health economist at the International Vaccine Access Center at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, who helped develop the tool, said in an interview.

Measles outbreaks can trigger a costly public health response, even when many people who are exposed never become sick.

When a case is identified, public health officials must investigate the infection, identify and notify people who may have been exposed, coordinate with hospitals and schools, and sometimes organize vaccination campaigns to prevent further spread. Those efforts can divert staff and resources from other public health priorities.

Costs could differ heavily depending on the state.

Johns Hopkins' data includes national averages alongside state-specific inputs, including inpatient hospital costs and wages from government data or trusted health policy sources. In New York, for example, the average inpatient hospital stay is roughly $4,200 a day, while the minimum wage is $16 per hour.

The tool estimates that the 48 cases reported by New York in 2025 carried an estimated $1.1 million in costs, including nearly $749,000 in public health responses, $333,000 in productivity losses and roughly $24,500 in direct medical expenses.

Meanwhile, the 803 cases in Texas, the epicenter of the largest U.S. measles outbreak in 2025, carried estimated costs of nearly $13 million, according to the tool. That includes $9 million in public health costs, $4 million in productivity losses and around $288,000 in direct medical expenses.

The tool builds on a study published by Patenaude and other researchers in August, which examined the costs of measles outbreaks in the U.S. The researchers reviewed published studies and government reports to compile historical estimates of medical expenses and public health response costs across 18 states.

The paper estimated that outbreaks carried a historical average cost of nearly $59,000 per case, just from the public health response. The researchers also estimated how costs scale with outbreak size, separating fixed up-front response expenses from the additional costs associated with each new case.

"A lot of this is incurred up front, and that's because of the human labor that's involved in contact tracing and all of the different actors that sort of need to be mobilized to deal with the fact that there's an outbreak," Patenaude told CNBC. "It's a largely labor-driven cost."

That model estimated roughly $298,000 in fixed costs and about $16,300 for each additional case. But it relied on historical data, including a particularly large 2019 outbreak in New York City, where contact-tracing labor costs were high. The researchers also excluded productivity losses from that model because most of the historical studies they reviewed did not consistently account for them.

The economic burden of measles extends beyond the costs of treating infected patients and organizing a public health response.

People who are exposed to measles may have to quarantine, even if they never develop symptoms. Parents can also be forced to miss work to care for sick or exposed children, while workers who contract the disease may lose wages during their recovery.

A 2025 study by Joey Mattingly, a pharmacist and health economist at the University of Utah, used published epidemiological and cost data to model the potential economic burden of a hypothetical 1,500-case outbreak. His analysis included direct medical expenses, public health spending and lost productivity, and examined how the estimates changed under different assumptions, including hospitalization rates.

The study estimated that the hypothetical outbreak could cost about $90 million, including roughly $26 million in productivity losses.

Those indirect costs can add up quickly because measles is highly contagious and a single infected person can expose dozens of others. Those contacts may need to quarantine or miss work while health officials determine whether they have been infected, creating costs even when they never develop the disease.

Mattingly estimated that each person who is quarantined could amount to roughly $300 in lost productivity alone.

If a single measles case exposes 40 to 50 people who then need to quarantine, that could translate into an estimated $12,000 to $15,000 in lost productivity, even if none of those contacts become sick.

That ripple effect is one reason Mattingly argues that the economic costs of outbreaks can quickly outweigh the investment required to vaccinate people against measles.

"Even if you don't get the infection, the contact costs you," he said. "And so it's just showing that there's this multiplication or exponential growth on the economic side that happens."

The financial burden can also fall unevenly across communities. While the costs of responding to an outbreak may be shared among local, state and federal agencies, Mattingly said the impact is often felt directly by local health departments.

"It's not like your health department is sitting around waiting for measles outbreaks," he said. "They're working, doing all these other things, and imagine having to just drop what you're doing because there's been a huge outbreak in the community."

Patenaude said the findings could help state and local health departments better anticipate the resources needed to contain outbreaks — particularly as measles cases continue to rise.

"It's not super expensive to treat and deal with a single case of measles compared to a single case of cancer," he said. "But if you have these outbreaks popping up and you're having to constantly divert resources that could be spent elsewhere by state and local health departments on measles, then you're going to wind up spending a lot of money very quickly."

#412. 3 Best Walking Pad Treadmills for Homes and Offices (2026)

Unlike a bulky and almost always noisy treadmill, the best walking pad is a slim (ideally portable) machine that can slide under your standing desk or fit anywhere in your home. You can rack up steps while working, binge-watching reality TV, or just avoiding the looming winter. Sitting at your desk all day is literally a pain. It’s terrible for your posture and takes a toll on your physical and mental health; I know this because I'm currently typing this as a hunched-over keyboard gargoyle. Theoretically, you could take walking breaks away from your screen, but even then, you’re still spending a lot of the day on your tush.

I’m also convinced that walking pads make me more productive. Not only do you crush your daily steps before 5 pm, but daily use can boost your energy levels, mood, and cardiovascular health. Walking on an incline also strengthens and tones your lower-body muscles, so you can even get a decent workout in. There are tons on the market, offering varying speed capabilities, extra features like built-in desk platforms and wheels, and durability. Our remote testers clocked serious hours walking, working, and sometimes jogging to find the best walking pads worth the space and money.

For more WFH gear recommendations, check out Best Standing Desks, Best Laptop Stands, and Best Office Chairs.

Updated November 2026: I’ve added the FlexiSpot MotionX Ultra Auto Incline Walking Treadmill, Merach UltraWalk W60, and the Umay U20F Folding 3-in-1 Walking Pad. I've also added more walking pads to avoid. Product information, prices, and links were also updated.

What Features Should I Look for in a Walking Pad?

Size and storage: Walking pads are typically much smaller than traditional treadmills, but check the product dimensions and make sure the footprint will work for you. If you’re working with an especially small space, consider a foldable or under-desk walking pad. If you’re planning to store it under furniture or in a closet when not in use, shop for slimmer models with wheels for portability.

Belt size and track length: The deck size is dependent on the product’s dimensions, and you should take into consideration how much space you have to work with. But, generally, I prefer wider and lengthier belts that feel more stable to walk on, especially if you’re on the taller side or have a longer stride.

Weight capacity: Walking pads aren’t as durable as treadmills and tend to have a lower weight capacity. Check the machine’s limit, and make sure it will work for you and anyone else in the household who will be using it. Per the online reviews we’ve read, walking pads are notorious for falling apart after only a few years or even a few months of use, so it might be worth getting something that’s a little more heavy-duty.

Speed range: Choose a walking pad that offers a speed range that best fits your lifestyle and goals. Most models will generally offer speeds up to 4 mph, which should cover everything from gentle strolls and brisk walks, and is ideal for home office use. There are walking pad-treadmill hybrids that offer faster paces if you’re also looking for a jog.

Adjustable inclines: Some models remain flat, but I prefer the walking pads that feature adjustable levels so that you can customize your cardio intensity as you see fit. Walking at an incline can also strengthen your lower body muscles and reduce anterior knee loading.

Metrics and display: Most walking pads have an LED display that tracks your steps, distance, time, and calories. Some models also include a remote control and/or app, which can sometimes sync your metrics from the machine, so you can check your workout data on your phone. This may not matter to you if you prefer to use your Apple Watch or fitness tracker.

Price: You can snag a solid, no-frills walking pad for under $300, but if you’re looking for a higher-end model with a surplus of features, it can cost $500+.

Our remote team members evaluated each walking pad from their own home offices, using the pads during real workdays. Testers of different heights, stride lengths, and lifestyles walked for hour-plus stretches while answering emails, taking meetings, and performing other work-related tasks; we noted how each pad felt over time and whether any annoyances surfaced with prolonged use. We evaluated every model for at least two weeks across the following criteria:

I’ll be testing the DeerRun Q2 Urban Smart Walking Pad and the Urevo Strol 2E. I’m also reevaluating Urevo CyberPad for Home.

Most walking pads have similar capabilities, but the Urevo CyberPad stands out. It offers a 14-percent incline, which is one of the highest inclines on the market. WIRED contributing reviewer Kristin Canning loved that she could tackle steeper walks to get in a real workout without sacrificing her work productivity. She’s confident in the product’s durability, thanks to its solid feel underfoot and comfortable belt. It was ready to use right out of the box and painless to set up. It weighs about 71 pounds, so moving it around isn’t fun, but the wheels help.

You can control the CyberPad (and the colorful lights along the side rails) with its Bluetooth remote and app. As long as you open the app at some point during your walk, it’ll log your workout data from the machine, so you can keep track of daily step counts over time. You can also view your current workout metrics on a screen at the front of the machine.

The speed tops out at 4 mph, and the incline stops at level nine, which can be either a 9- or 14-percent incline, depending on how you position the machine’s feet. Unfortunately, the incline capability does make it difficult to store under furniture. Here’s the full review.

This is a much longer and heavier walking pad; it’s 57 inches (32.5 inches when folded), which means it’s a good choice for taller folks or anyone who has a long stride. The deck feels stable, though not as springy or as padded as the CyberPad. You can adjust the speed via the remote control or companion app, but you don't need to set up the app to use the walking pad. You can check your time, speed, distance, calories, and steps on the large display.

Unfortunately, this machine was a bit of a pain to set up. Getting the Bluetooth remote and KS Fit app to connect was a struggle. I also don’t like that when you first start using the machine, you have to spend time walking at slower speeds before you can “unlock” the upper end of the range. The app itself is a little wonky; if you don’t start a workout with the app, your progress won’t be tracked or logged, and it doesn’t sync later. It also saves each walking session separately, so if you take a break, you start from zero again.

The app also offers workouts, which auto-adjust the speed to your pace via sensors under the belt. It felt awkward, since I couldn’t figure out how to slow the pace without the device totally stopping. You switch between modes within the app or on the display, and you can stop and start the machine using the app or remote. I like to use the machine in manual mode, and I tend to use the app more than the remote, because the remote connectivity is inconsistent.

The machine feels like it will have a long lifespan. Its heft makes it harder to move around, but its foldability is a win in general. Here’s my full review. —Kristin Canning

The Urevo Strol 2S Pro doubles as a treadmill and feels shockingly stable underfoot for how relatively lightweight it feels. It remained firmly in place even as I ran on it at top speed and incline (7.6 mph and 9 percent). That top speed isn’t fast enough if you’re looking to really sprint, but it’s definitely ample for a good workout. I really appreciated the quiet motor. Overall, it seems like a durable machine that offers great value, especially with the speed range.

If you want to use the treadmill as a walking pad, you can keep the handrail down, which limits the top speed to 4 mph (though you can still use the full incline functionality). There’s a display on the front of the machine that cycles between time, distance, calories, incline, and speed. To access faster speeds, you have to put the handrail up, which requires you to use a hex tool to tighten it into place. The handlebar has a display as well, and you can control the machine’s basic functions from there. There’s also a tablet holder you can attach.

The machine was very easy to set up, and the remote and app connected to it right away. Its companion app was my favorite of those I tested—it was intuitive, showed the info I wanted to see in my profile (like daily step counts), and synced well with the machine. There are some wonky translations, but nothing that made it hard to use. As long as you connect to your treadmill at some point during your walk (even if you start and control the machine outside the app), it will log your workout data. In the app, you can use the Quick Start function to walk freely and adjust the settings from your phone, or you can do a virtual walk with iconic scenery. —Kristin Canning

FlexiSpot MotionX Ultra Auto Incline Walking Treadmill for $370: This walking pad offers a solid incline range, going up to a level nine, or a 12-percent incline. The 39.4-inch track felt stable underfoot, even at the highest incline and max speed (4 mph). It’s the quietest walking pad I’ve tested. You can connect your phone to play music through the speakers, too. The remote allows you to take on 30-minute, preprogrammed walking workouts, too, where the machine auto-adjusts the speed throughout (though not the incline). Otherwise, you can use it in manual mode and adjust the settings to your liking. Unfortunately, it doesn’t track step count.

Merach UltraWalk W60 for $400: At 67.7 pounds, this walking pad is heavy, making setup right out of the box practically a two-person job. So it's best to choose a permanent spot for it. If you do need to relocate it, though, built-in wheels ease the effort. The upside to its sturdy frame is that it remains steady underfoot, regardless of the incline or speed level. Connecting it to your TV is simple and features a display overlay that tracks your steps and elapsed time without distracting from your show. While it includes several preprogrammed workout routines, the lack of clear guidance makes speed and incline adjustments feel somewhat sudden. It also lacks handlebars, which is a drawback for anyone seeking extra support. Lastly, the paint began chipping after just two weeks of use.

Toputure TP6 Treadmill with Adjustable Handle for $299: This is a foldable, screenless walking pad that is ideal for small apartments and spaces. It takes only a few minutes to set up and compact, but it weighs 56 pounds, so it’s not the most portable treadmill. During my second day of testing, the pad began making a high-pitched scraping noise, which likely indicated a misaligned belt. This unbearable noise would come and go throughout my two weeks of testing. The handlebars are height-adjustable, ranging from 32 to 45 inches, and they are also tilt-adjustable. Be aware of the handlebar locks while walking by, as they can catch on clothing. It's a decent budget pick.

Umay U20F Folding 3-in-1 Walking Pad for $350: I always appreciate a walking pad that can pull double duty as a treadmill, and this sturdy, 44-pound model fits the bill: There’s a handlebar with tablet holder that can fold up if you want to run, with the walking surface adjustable to a 15 percent incline. However, due to the adjustable incline mechanism underneath, this pad is not able to sit all the way flat, which means it’s permanently at a 5-percent incline. This made it too tall for some standing desks, too tiring to walk on for long periods during the workday, and challenging to store compared with other walking pads. —Kat Merck

Urevo SpaceWalk 5L Smart Walking Pad for $400: The SpaceWalk 5L is decent and cheaper than the CyberPad, but I still prefer the latter for its 14 percent incline and the Strol 2S Pro for its speed and ability to double as a treadmill. The SpaceWalk 5L has a larger weight capacity, maxing out at 300 pounds, with the same incline as the Strol 2S Pro (9 percent) and max speed as the CyberPad (4 mph). Its track length is a solid 39 inches, and WIRED contributor Michael Venutolo-Mantovani said it’s compact enough to stash underneath a couch or loveseat.

Egofit Walker Pro M1 for $399: This is one of the first walking pads we tested, and it has a fixed 5 percent incline that might pose some storage issues (you won’t be able to just slide it under the couch). It can be controlled via an app or a remote, but it doesn’t automatically sync if you use the remote. But it’s lightweight and easy to move around your home.

GoYouth 2-in-1 Under Desk Treadmill for $265: This is an Amazon top pick, but it’s very bare-bones for this price. There’s no app, and the remote and controls are unintuitive. The built-in Bluetooth speaker sounds unsurprisingly crappy. But it does feel great underfoot, is very quiet, and has a higher weight capacity (300 pounds) and a slightly longer belt than the Sperax. If you’re a larger person looking for a simple, affordable walking pad, this will work for you. —Adrienne So

Lifesmart TM2202 3-in-1 Treadmill for $699: The 3-in-1 Treadmill gets its name from its ability to serve as a walking pad (with the rail down, the speed tops out at 3.7 mph), a treadmill (with the rail up, you can run up to 7.5 mph), and a workstation (with the rail up and desk attachment on) for multitasking. It’s easy to set up, and switching between modes is quick and simple (you just need to use a small tool to adjust the rail; the tool is magnetized, so it can live on the rail and never get lost in a junk drawer). The desk’s height won’t accommodate everyone; it was lower than I’d like, and I’m 5'4". But for basic tasks like answering emails or sitting in on a meeting, it was just fine. It also doesn’t have any incline options, though. —Kristin Canning

NordicTrack Commercial 1750 Treadmill for $2,500: With a top speed of 12 mph, incline and decline options, a springy running deck, fans, a 16-inch pivoting touchscreen, and two water bottle holders, this is the best treadmill tester Kristin Canning has used. Although it is a foldable treadmill and suitable for walking and hiking workouts, it’s still bulky and relatively expensive if you’re only looking for a space-saving walking pad. If you’re searching for a running treadmill, check out Kristin’s guide to the Best Home Treadmills.

Sperax Walking Pad: The US Consumer Product Safety Commission has warned consumers to stop using several Sperax walking pad and treadmill models due to the fall and fire hazards they pose. According to the CPSC, the machines can unexpectedly change speeds or come to a sudden stop, and there have also been reports of overheating and fires. The warning applies to the Pro, Q1, RM-01, and RM-02 models, but I’d avoid this brand altogether.

GoPlus 2-in-1 Folding Treadmill for $170: There were just too many detractors for us to recommend this budget-friendly walking pad, although we hope to test some newer versions from the same company. The 3-foot power cord made it difficult to find a good spot for the pad since it needed to be so close to the wall. It was too bulky to transport around the house or store under furniture. The included remote was small and easy to lose, and the included battery died quickly. And there were no on-board buttons to control the treadmill, so if you lose the remote, you'll have an expensive paperweight until you can replace it. I also found the phone holder too small and the shipping box poorly designed. While the low price is appealing, and I liked the folding handles and general feel of the belt, you’d be better off investing in one of our picks. —Louryn Strampe

#411. JetBlue unveils new first-class seats to bring back profits

JetBlue Airways unveiled its new domestic first-class seats Wednesday as more of the country's carriers bet on the premium travel boom to make them profitable again.

JetBlue announced it would offer a domestic first class — BlueFirst — in December 2024 and plans to start flying aircraft with the new seats and full on-board product next month. The carrier showed off the new cabin at its home hub at John F. Kennedy International Airport on Wednesday. Retrofits will continue during 2027, CEO Joanna Geraghty told CNBC late last month.

JetBlue will have 12 of the seats on its Airbus A320 fleet and 30 extra-legroom seats, down from 42 of those options previously. Because the airplanes don't have ovens, JetBlue said it is offering an array of cold food options like sesame noodles, pastrami sandwiches and tortellini salad, though they vary by route.

The rollout comes as the New York airline tries to stabilize after last posting a profit in 2019. Last month, JetBlue executives sought to calm investors after shares in the airline hit a record low following a report that bondholders had contracted restructuring advisors.

"We have no intention, no plans to file for bankruptcy. We are not engaged with any restructuring company," Geraghty told CNBC in an interview on Oct. 27.

Geraghty told CNBC's Phil LeBeau on Wednesday that JetBlue has "met with a few of the creditors over the last couple of weeks as part of ... normal investor engagement." She said the company made it clear to bondholders that JetBlue doesn't need to reorganize.

The airline's shares are up more than 40% this month, more than other passenger carriers, as of midday Wednesday, though they are still off about 11% so far this year.

JetBlue is betting that resilient travel demand, especially for roomier seats will help fuel its turnaround plan, which aims to get the carrier to an operating profit next year and $1 in adjusted earnings per share in 2028.

Airline executives have said they expect fares to stay elevated next year as they grapple with higher costs, including but not limited to fuel, such as labor and airport fees. Geraghty said Wednesday that even if fuel prices moderate next year, "I don't think fares are going to fall as much as people expect."

Airlines large and small are racing to outfit their cabins with roomier, more expensive seating options to help cover ever-higher costs, from jet fuel to labor to airport operation fees.

For example, a ticket in the new recliner first-class seats between Kennedy airport and Fort Lauderdale-Hollywood International Airport in Florida is going for $847 roundtrip in the first week of March while standard economy is $446.

On Tuesday, budget carrier Frontier Airlines unveiled its new first-class seats, which will take up the first two rows, and said it would outfit its Airbus fleet with premium seating that has extra legroom and in-seat power through row eight. The airline will take out a row of seating on its planes to make the pricier ones fit, Chief Marketing Officer Bobby Schroeter told CNBC in an interview Monday.

JetBlue last month reported that it ended the third quarter with $2.2 billion in liquidity and that its unencumbered asset base is now $7 billion, including $2.8 billion in aircraft and engines.

JetBlue had total debt of $8.4 billion as of Sept. 30, the carrier said in its earnings filing.

Geraghty told CNBC last month there is a "fundamental disconnect between the narrative in the bond market and what our financial results actually demonstrate."

When asked whether JetBlue's eye on keeping costs down includes furloughs, layoffs or buyouts, Geraghty last month said "absolutely not."

#410. OpenAI launches ChatGPT Wallet, claiming its a safe way to have AI shop for you

Would you trust an AI agent to make purchases online on your behalf? AI companies are hoping you will.

However, many e-commerce and financial service companies are skeptical of the technology and have blocked AI agents from using their platforms.

On Friday, OpenAI launched a new product called ChatGPT Wallet that appears to be an attempt to address that skepticism.

According to OpenAI, ChatGPT Wallet will let AI agents make purchases for users without sharing their credit card information each time. In addition, "security checks" are required for every purchase an AI agent makes. This entails a human user manually reviewing and approving every purchase.

"Users add a card once, then approve each purchase without re-entering their payment details," according to ChatGPT. "Before each purchase, the user reviews the items, total, and selected card, then approves it with a security check such as Face ID or a fingerprint scan."

Instead of revealing credit card information to vendors, ChatGPT said it will purchase items using a payment token that serves as a substitute for the actual card's details. The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Additionally, ChatGPT Wallet users can still take advantage of card rewards programs and purchase dispute protections while utilizing ChatGPT Wallet.

Most notably, OpenAI says that ChatGPT partnered with the big three credit card companies — American Express, Mastercard, and Visa.

E-commerce giant Amazon notably blocked AI agents, such as Meta's Muse, from accessing its platform in September due to security concerns. Banks like JPMorgan Chase followed suit and also blocked Muse and other AI agents. And, just this week, United Airlines blocked AI agents from booking flights due to similar security concerns.

In addition to launching ChatGPT Wallet with major credit card company partnerships, OpenAI is releasing "tools and guidance" for businesses to encourage support for shopping AI agents.

OpenAI seems to want to build trust among retailers and financial institutions first, as the company says it plans to allow users to delegate purchases entirely to their AI agents while adhering to a set list of rules and parameters.

ChatGPT Wallet rolls out today in dots, OpenAI's AI agent platform, for users with a ChatGPT Pro subscription in the U.S.

However, as OpenAI notes, ChatGPT Wallet is very much in a trial stage as it analyzes how businesses respond to AI agents making purchases. In addition, ChatGPT Wallet will not work with websites that block AI agents.

#408. American Airlines brings fresh food back to NY-Chicago first class for first time since 2019

American Airlines said Wednesday it's bringing small, fresh "bites" to first class on flights between its hubs in New York and Chicago, the carrier's latest effort to win over higher-spending customers.

The changes start this month. Customers will be able to pick between two options, which include prosciutto and melon or apple cinnamon chia pudding for breakfast and a Thai noodle salad or mezze plate for lunch and dinner, American said Wednesday.

American last offered fresh food for first class on these routes in 2019, a spokeswoman said. Currently, customers in first class on these flights get a smaller, packaged snack. Airlines largely have limited food options for shorter flights, even in first class.

American has fallen behind Delta Air Lines and United Airlines in profits and investments in premium options. The airline has been working to step up those premium offerings in recent years with new cabins, lounges and plans for seatback screens on its narrow-body fleets.

#407. Microsoft outage was largest of 2026, report says

As Microsoft continues to try to fix a huge service outage that has disrupted software services globally, a new report dropped, possibly confirming something most of us could have guessed.

Ookla and DownDetector published a report on Friday afternoon claiming that the Microsoft outage was, by a significant margin, the largest such outage of 2026, based on the number of users who reported problems on DownDetector. Going by that metric, Microsoft's outage was reported 7.4 million times, compared to just 2.8 million reports for a YouTube outage in February that ranked second among all outages in 2026.

During the Microsoft outage, DownDetector said that it received a peak of 19,650 user error reports for Microsoft's services per minute, which is more than 1,500 times the normal level. Nearly 24 hours later, users are still reporting errors for Microsoft

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Aside from the sheer number of reports, the length of the outage was also noteworthy. Outlook and other Microsoft enterprise software services were out for more than 24 hours, compared to a recent 11-hour outage in August, as Mashable reported at the time.

There is one major caveat to all of this: DownDetector relies on user reports rather than anything more verifiable. This has caused companies to sometimes dispute the site's data, which is exactly what Amazon Web Services did (rather passively aggressively) after DownDetector incorrectly showed that AWS was experiencing problems at the same time that Outlook was on Thursday. Mashable reached out to Microsoft, but the company declined to comment on the Ookla report.

As of late Friday afternoon, Microsoft's outage appears to be subsiding, though the company has yet to publish an ETA for full recovery.

#406. Jensen Huang and the End of the Founder Fairy Tale

It was July of 2024, and Jensen Huang was punchy.

I had agreed to travel to Denver, Colorado, to interview the Nvidia CEO at SIGGRAPH, the annual confab of computer graphics PhDs and practitioners. Early that year I’d conducted a lengthy sit-down interview with Huang for a WIRED magazine feature, which apparently went well enough—both for WIRED and for Nvidia’s executive communications team—that I had been asked to interview Huang again at this conference. Another opportunity to grill the CEO of the modern world’s most consequential company? Yes, of course. I began preparing.

What ensued was, in a word, bizarre. During the first prep call for the conference, the type of call-in which PR handlers typically try to coax specific questions out of journalists to prevent an ambush, a youngish, vaguely familiar woman took the lead. This was Madison Huang, Jensen’s daughter. She was clearly intelligent, commanding respect. She told me about the slides Jensen would be presenting on stage at SIGGRAPH and offered suggestions for key moments when I might tee him up to talk about the slides.

Slides? Tee up Jensen? What kind of “interview” was this?

I raised my concerns with both my editors and Nvidia. We agreed that I would ask the questions I intended to ask him, which included questions about the environmental impact of data centers, and that Huang would also be referring to slides. Let’s just say the interview didn’t go smoothly. (It’s on YouTube.) To this day, former and current Nvidia workers sometimes tell me that I “handled” the whole thing well. Huang was domineering, evaded some of my questions, and, immediately after our session concluded, did an impromptu Q&A session with a much more adoring crowd than me. Then I was guided offstage, and Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg hopped on, where he and Huang had a lovey-dovey fireside chat.

As a longtime journalist I’m used to occasional adversarial interactions; I really don’t lose sleep over them. This was just a CEO being a CEO. Huang later agreed to meet with me again, in December 2024. But that interview in Denver had underscored for me how much Jensen Huang wanted to control the narrative. Not just wanted to; he assumed he would. And to a large extent he did. Until this week.

Narrative has always been a critical part of Huang’s success. He was not just your average electrical engineering nerd who toiled away on something that would eventually find a product-market fit. By Huang’s own telling, he was an immigrant founder, an underdog, a wife guy who chased (and eventually married) his college sweetheart, a scrappy startup guy who dined at Denny’s in the early days. A visionary who worked Sundays and who didn’t sleep well because he always heard the hoof steps of competitors. A parent who brought his kids into the corporate fold. A man who loved Nvidia so much he tattooed an abstract version of the company logo on his arm.

Huang built the highest-valued publicly traded company in history. That part’s not just fairy tale. It’s true. Now, it’s starting to read more like a Greek tragedy.

Back in January 2024, when I sat down with him for our first-ever in-person meeting, Huang right off the bat talked about the importance of storytelling. Here’s an excerpt:

Jensen Huang: Somebody who I really admire, as a leader and a person, is Shantanu Narayen, the CEO of Adobe. He said he always wanted to be a journalist because he loved telling stories.

WIRED: It seems like an important part of building a business, being able to tell its story effectively.

Yes. Strategy setting is storytelling. Culture building is storytelling.

You’ve said many times you didn’t sell the idea of Nvidia based on a pitch deck.

That’s right. It was really about telling the story.

Of course, even then, Huang was caught up in the crosshairs of the US–China AI war and at the mercy of regulators, as I wrote at the time. After Donald Trump’s reelection that year, Huang once again deployed his storytelling skills and lobbied the Trump administration to loosen export controls, so he could sell some of his company’s chips to China.

Another crucial part of the Huang lore is that he ran a “flat” organization, with a few dozen top executives reporting to him. (This is sometimes reported as being “60” people; insiders have told me at times it’s closer to 35.) Huang once said the structure allowed Nvidia “to adapt a lot more quickly, which we need because our technology is moving so quickly.” Huang was also known to be controlling of all aspects of the business—participating in every significant meeting, seemingly aware of every moving part, and staying in the loop on sourcing, supply chains, and the company’s allocation of chips.

This is where Huang’s cult of leadership, and his Nvidia storytelling, may have come to a stupidly disappointing final chapter. As one former employee put it, Huang can’t simultaneously have had knowledge of every element of Nvidia’s business and then potentially feign ignorance about where the chips may have fallen, both figuratively and literally. His apparent lapses in judgment have now led him to resign from the very company he coaxed into existence and have shattered the mythos of its ever-prescient founder.

It’s certainly not the narrative ending Huang would have chosen, were he still in control.
