#369. The FAA nationwide ground stop sparks travel panic and viral memes

Update: The ground stop has been lifted, and domestic flights resumed late Friday.

For only the third time in U.S. history, flights across the United States have been ordered to stay on the ground. And as travelers found themselves stranded at airports, sitting in airport bars, and wondering when they might finally take off, the internet did what it does best and started posting.

On Friday, Nov. 13, the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) issued a nationwide ground stop for departing flights following widespread air traffic control communications problems. The agency cited extraordinary circumstances and safety concerns, ordering aircraft to remain on the ground until further notice. Flights already in the air were permitted to land, while inbound international flights were being handled on a case-by-case basis.

The scale of the disruption is difficult to overstate. The last time the FAA issued a nationwide ground stop was in January 2023, when a computer system failure temporarily halted departing flights. Before that, it happened in the aftermath of the Sept. 11, 2001, terrorist attacks.

It's the kind of historical comparison that can make an already unsettling situation feel even more alarming, particularly when thousands of travelers are left waiting for answers. The FAA's original communication was also extremely vague, citing only "extraordinary circumstances" and "reasons of safety."

And on social media, people had plenty of questions.

For travelers caught in the middle of the disruption, the news wasn't just another breaking headline. It meant unexpected delays, uncertain departure times, and an increasingly familiar feeling of being at the mercy of systems entirely outside their control.

Some turned to social media to document the experience in real time, sharing updates from airport terminals.

Others were struck by the sheer rarity of the moment. A nationwide ground stop is not an everyday occurrence, and the fact that the previous two were associated with 9/11 and a major FAA systems failure only added to the unease.

Of course, this being the internet, it didn't take long for people to find humor in the situation, either. Even an unprecedented disruption to air travel isn't immune to a few jokes, particularly when it happens on Friday the 13th following a major Microsoft outage.

And then, there's this response from Stephen King:

But beyond the immediate frustration and online commentary, the ground stop raises more serious questions about the aging technology underpinning America's aviation system. The FAA has been working to modernize its air traffic control infrastructure, and Friday's communications problems offer another reminder of how much depends on systems most travelers rarely think about until something goes wrong.

The ground stop also comes at a particularly interesting moment for the FAA. Just days earlier, the agency announced that it was expanding its new AI-supported air traffic management system, known as SMART, to all 22 regional air traffic control centers nationwide. Shortly before that, the FAA announced it will finally phase out floppy disks by 2028.

The $875 million system is designed to analyze everything from weather patterns and flight routes to traffic congestion and controller staffing, helping aviation officials anticipate disruptions before they happen. According to the FAA, the technology is intended to reduce delays and make air travel more efficient, though human specialists remain responsible for reviewing its recommendations.

The timing has attracted attention online, particularly as questions swirl around what caused Friday's communications problems.

For now, travelers are left refreshing their flight statuses, waiting for updates, and sharing their experiences online. And while the jokes might offer a momentary distraction, there's nothing particularly funny about realizing just how quickly an entire country's air travel system can grind to a halt.

#366. More autonomy, fewer NDAs: Volunteers have new requests for brain implant trials

People with experimental brain implants are pushing for more recognition and respect.

Scientists have been working on ways to decode brain activity for decades. Teams have developed brain implants that can be used to control robotic limbs and computers, and even to “speak.”

The people who volunteer for trials of those devices play a vital role in that research, and they ought to be recognized for their contributions, says Ian Burkhart, who had one implanted in his own brain back in 2014. He’s been working on a set of guidelines for researchers developing brain-computer interfaces (BCIs).

Ethics guidelines for clinical trials already exist. But BCI trials are different, says Jennifer Chandler, a professor of law and neuroethics at the University of Ottawa who also worked on the guidelines. “These are people who have a device embedded in their brain, are in trials sometimes for years and years … [and contribute] a significant investment of time, of the body, and of intellectual effort.”

Burkhart was left paralyzed from the chest down following a diving accident in 2010, when he was 19 years old. Around four years later, he volunteered to become the first person to trial a brain implant designed for hand control.

He had an array of 96 electrodes inserted into the part of his brain that controls movement. The electrodes were connected to a computer via a cable that essentially plugged into his head. Algorithms on the computer were designed to interpret those signals and deliver them to another sleeve of electrodes worn on Burkhart’s arm, allowing him to control it.

The first movements were small, but Burkhart was ultimately able to use the system to pour beads from bottles, move small and delicate objects, and even play Guitar Hero, if only during lab visits. It was a scientific breakthrough.

Burkhart took part in two years-long clinical trials over the seven-and-a-half-year period he had the electrode array implanted in his brain. “It was a fantastic experience,” he says. “But there’s certainly some things that, knowing what I know now, I would want to do a little differently.”

In 2022, Burkhart cofounded BCI Pioneers Coalition—an organization through which people who have taken part in BCI trials can share their experiences, both with each other and with researchers. Over the last year, members of the group have been developing a set of guidelines based on those experiences. The idea is to help academics, clinicians, and companies run ethical trials that respect participants’ autonomy.

The guidelines, published on Tuesday, lay out a list of recommendations that fall within 13 categories. One, for example, focuses on recognizing and compensating trial participants. During the trials that Burkhart participated in, “I was going into the lab two or three days a week, for four hours at a time,” he recalls. “That’s [easily] a part-time job if you just count the hours you’re putting in.”

As the trial progressed, Burkhart says, he learned more and was able to give more constructive feedback on what was working and what wasn’t. He says he was a key member of the research team. Others who find themselves in a similar position might appreciate being listed as a coauthor on scientific publications or publicly named as a research collaborator, he says. Burkhart and his colleagues acknowledge that not everyone will want this, however.

The guidelines state that brain data “should be understood as the participant’s personal information.” They are pushing for participants to be able to access their own brain data whenever they want—something that has not always been possible—and want to make sure people aren’t prevented from sharing it with their own doctors because of embargoes or nondisclosure agreements (NDAs) designed to keep findings under wraps from competitors.

“It’s not super common for there to be firm NDAs within a lot of the trials,” says Burkhart. “But it’s something where we want to make sure that that does not happen.”

The guidelines also address what comes after a trial. Around five years after Burkhart had his device implanted, the research team ran into some issues with funding. When we last spoke a few years ago, he recalled how he was told he would have to have the implant removed and could have it put back in once the team had secured funding. (And we’re talking about brain surgery here.)

Ultimately, he kept the implant until 2021, when it was removed after an infection started to develop at the point where the cable led into his scalp. But losing the device after all those years was difficult; he’d regained the ability to move his arm—and then lost it again.

Rita Leggett, an Australian woman who had a device implanted in her brain to treat her epilepsy, has said she felt she “became one” with her implant. When the company that made it went bust two years later, the device was removed against her will—an act ethicists have described as a potential breach of human rights.

Researchers should instead commit to maintaining brain devices for a “reasonable period” after a trial ends, according to the guidelines. Participants should be given plenty of time to prepare for the surgery to remove it. And researchers should set aside extra funds to support trial participants who have to do that.

Some of the guidelines are vague and may overlap with existing ethics standards. They don’t all spell out explicit solutions. They were also developed with feedback from a small group of individuals—the number of people living with BCIs is still very low. The team hopes to grow and expand on the guidelines over time. Burkhart also plans to discuss them with researchers at an upcoming neuroscience conference.

“This is a living document, and this is version one,” says Chandler. “It’s just the starting point.”

#365. Moon phase today: What the Moon will look like on November 14, 2026

The Moon is getting a little bit bigger each night as we work through the lunar phases. We're still in the Waxing Crescent phase, which means more of the Moon is becoming illuminated each evening as it makes its way towards the First Quarter

As of Saturday, Nov. 14, the NASA's Daily Moon Guide tracker tells us the Moon is in the Waxing Crescent phase, with 22% of its surface visible.

If you don't have any visual aids, you can see see some features on the Moon's surface tonight, specifically the Mare Crisium and the Mare Fecunditatis. With binoculars or a telescope, you'll also see the Endymion Crater.

The next Full Moon will take place on November 24.

The Moon takes approximately 29.5 days to complete one full cycle. As it orbits Earth, the amount of sunlight reflecting off its surface that we can see changes, creating the different phases. This takes us from the New Moon to the Full Moon and back again, before the cycle begins once more, according to NASA.

New Moon - The Moon is between Earth and the sun, so the side we see is dark (in other words, it's invisible to the eye).

Waxing Crescent - A small sliver of light appears on the right side (Northern Hemisphere).

First Quarter - Half of the Moon is lit on the right side. It looks like a half-Moon.

Waxing Gibbous - More than half is lit up, but it's not quite full yet.

Full Moon - The whole face of the Moon is illuminated and fully visible.

Waning Gibbous - The Moon starts losing light on the right side. (Northern Hemisphere)

Third Quarter (or Last Quarter) - Another half-Moon, but now the left side is lit.

Waning Crescent - A thin sliver of light remains on the left side before going dark again.

#362. The Download: Silicon Valley’s blind spot and Nvidia’s chip-tracking plan

Plus: A major Microsoft outage has disrupted services worldwide.

This is today's edition of The Download, our weekday newsletter that provides a daily dose of what's going on in the world of technology.

—Aaron Zamost, a tech communications consultant and a former executive at Square

The self-styled visionaries of Silicon Valley have built careers claiming to see the future. Yet five weeks before the midterms, many gathered at the White House to pose with Trump and endorse his “Super Intelligence” agenda, despite growing opposition to AI and data centers.

Their decision to lash themselves to President Trump was a catastrophic misreading of the political landscape. Now, not only have they jeopardized their companies’ fates, but they’ve also raised questions about their vision and judgment.

Here’s why Silicon Valley’s tech leaders may be less visionary than they think.

Nvidia representatives told Illinois representative Bill Foster that it was possible to implement the anti-smuggling measures called for in the Chip Security Act, Foster told MIT Technology Review on Thursday, despite an industry group claiming they were “potentially infeasible.”

Foster, a Democrat who serves on the Committee on Science, Space, and Technology, is a cosponsor of the act, which would introduce geotracking requirements to verify the location of advanced AI chips and prevent their smuggling to China.

The act is drawing renewed attention following Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s resignation and reported affair. Find out what the proposed tracking measures would involve.

The people who volunteer for trials of these devices play a vital role in that research, and they ought to be recognized for their contributions, says Ian Burkhart, who had one implanted in his own brain back in 2014.

He’s been working on a set of guidelines for ethical trials to respect participants’ autonomy—and access their own brain data whenever they want.

#361. Hurdle hints and answers for November 14, 2026

If you like playing daily word games like Wordle, then Hurdle is a great game to add to your routine.

There are five rounds to the game. The first round sees you trying to guess the word, with correct, misplaced, and incorrect letters shown in each guess. If you guess the correct answer, it'll take you to the next hurdle, providing the answer to the last hurdle as your first guess. This can give you several clues or none, depending on the words. For the final hurdle, every correct answer from previous hurdles is shown, with correct and misplaced letters clearly shown.

An important note is that the number of times a letter is highlighted from previous guesses does necessarily indicate the number of times that letter appears in the final hurdle.

#360. The FAA Nationwide Ground Stop Is Over

Instability in a system that allows communications between the Federal Aviation Administration, air traffic controllers, and pilots was responsible for the country’s most disastrous nationwide ground stop in air traffic since September 11. The ground stop lasted five hours.

Several outlets reported that a failure in the FAA Telecommunications Infrastructure or FTI network, was linked to the FAA’s decision to declare a ground stop. The FTI is the private communications backbone for the air traffic control system. The system is in the midst of a major, multi-year modernization project, which involves replacing a decades-old network of copper wire with high-speed fiber-optic cables. The FAA has said the update will reduce outages and outage-related delays, and make the system safer. It will also be equipped to more easily handle newer aircraft, including drones, electric vertical take-off and landing aircraft (eVTOLs), and other “air taxis.”

At the time the ground stop was lifted on Friday evening, over 6,300 flights within, into, or out of the US had been canceled, according to the aviation tech company FlightAware; 9,500 had been delayed. Freight and mail flights have also been affected. It will likely take days, if not weeks, for airlines to reposition aircraft and crew and get their schedules back on track.

Delays will continue, US Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said in a Friday evening briefing at the White House. “It will take us some time to unwind what has happened.” He blamed “old switches, old routers” and a “data overload” for the communications systems failure. He said a “legacy system”—likely referring to FTI—was involved.

The issue was caused by “putting patches into the system” as new equipment was being installed, US Department of Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin said at the White House briefing. “The new technology was overwhelming the older system,” he said.

Mullin said there were no indications that the outage was related to a cyberattack, but “there’s not complete certainty right now.”

The system is managed by the government contractor L3Harris Technologies. On its website, L3Harris says that its “team’s deep knowledge of the US national airspace allows us to upgrade systems without disrupting air traffic operations.” A spokesperson for the company did not respond to a request for comment.

In May, L3Harris said it had completed 50 percent of its modernization project. Seventy percent of FAA sites have been upgraded to fiber, the company said earlier this month. The Trump Administration has said it aims to overhaul the nation's Air Traffic Control System by the end of 2028.

In August, the FAA tapped AT&T to upgrade the FTI, the private FAA communication network, to an entirely new system. That contract is worth $73 million, though its worth is likely to climb into the billions over the years. Previously, Verizon had been selected to build and operate this new system. L2Harris is meant to operate the legacy system until the upgrades are in place.

Earlier Friday, before the ground stop, US Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy sent a letter to congressional leaders asking them to approve his department’s request for $30.1 billion in funding to further upgrade the air traffic control system, and to improve airports. On Friday, he upped the ask: “I want $50 billion,” he said during a briefing at the White House.

A September report published by the US Government Accountability Office (GAO) raised concerns that the FAA’s attempt to accelerate long-delayed modernization plans “could be prioritizing speed over costs and air traffic disruption.” It found that the FAA does not have a “well-documented” lifecycle cost estimate for its programs, and that, rather than a master schedule guiding the sprawling project, has 11,389 schedules that are not integrated.

#358. Google faces calls for AI moratoriums in schools after Gemini pilot backlash

Families confront Google after teen’s lawsuit labels Gemini a “suicide coach.”

CHICAGO—Families joined protests in seven cities nationwide on Wednesday, rallying against what they see as Google’s bid to entrench its AI models in schools, turn kids of all ages into Gemini guinea pigs, and hook them on AI.

The backlash follows a Google statement last week, confirming that there will be no way for students to use Google Search without AI. While Google claimed it was giving schools “more choice, not less”—by turning off Search, Gemini, and Gemini Notebook by default—it would not provide an option that allowed Search without AI. For students, the only way to avoid interacting with AI was to remember to add “-ai” to every query, parents worried.

In Chicago, many families showed up to protest Google, despite cold, rainy weather. Waving signs, dozens of protesters marched up to Google’s downtown office as workers seemed to be leaving for the day, shouting chants like “Hey Google, nice try, we don’t want your Gemini” and “Hey, Google, leave our kids alone!”

Called to action by a nonprofit grassroots advocacy group, the Illinois Families for Public Schools (ILFPS), some parents and students had previously won a major AI fight. In August, the group pressured Chicago Public Schools (CPS) to pull out of a Gemini pilot that would have roped in all high school students districtwide—without consulting parents. And now, ILFPS is demanding a moratorium on AI until risks to students are better understood and CPS can draft a policy to protect students from potential learning setbacks or more harmful outcomes from engaging Google’s AI bots. If the group wins, it will join cities like Los Angeles and New York City in cautiously banning AI in schools.

Most alarming to parents, Google now faces a lawsuit from a 13-year-old student in Durham, North Carolina named “Emma Doe.” As Emma has alleged, she was a happy, well-adjusted, straight-A student when she started using Gemini on her school-issued Chromebook. Emma’s parents had no idea she was using Gemini, as the lawsuit alleged that Google “deliberately” kept them in the dark while driving Emma into so-called “AI psychosis.” Over time, normal school-related interactions with the chatbot led to more intense chats, and ultimately the chatbot allegedly coached Emma to plan her own suicide and funeral. The school warned her parents and averted the danger after an internal system flagged a chat labeled “Funeral,” but Emma’s story stoked fears of parents joining protests not just in Durham, but also in Boston; Boulder, Colorado; Chicago; Los Angeles; New York City; and Portland, Oregon.

Emma’s lawsuit accuses Google of exploiting its dominant position in education technology and “sneaking” Gemini into Chromebooks, while falsely marketing them as safe.

“As a direct consequence of Google’s choices, Emma was baited by its defectively designed products into repeated, unmonitored, sycophantic AI chatbot interactions on her school Chromebook without her parents ever knowing. And, but for a fortunate turn of events, Google’s AI may well have manipulated Emma into taking her own life,” the lawsuit alleged. “Google must be held to account for treating the lives of children as disposable in service of its bottom line.”

Google has promised to “continue to engage deeply with educators to ensure we are giving schools more choice, more controls, and improved Search for learning.” However, Google’s statements so far attempting to address parents’ concerns focus exclusively on documented harms to learning. On that front, Google claims that it’s solving the problem of AI eroding students’ critical thinking skills by launching yet another pilot for students. In this pilot, students using AI Mode and AI Overviews will be offered a “step-by-step guided learning through problems, so that students don’t get quick answers to schoolwork,” Google said.

Cassie Creswell, executive director of ILFPS, told Ars that she’s been an advocate monitoring edtech issues for more than a decade. And even she was blindsided by Google’s AI moves.

“I am aware of AI Overviews when you search, which have a whole slew of problems, but I did not know that you really actually just have an interface where you can interact with Gemini in the chatbot way on everyone’s Chromebook still in Chicago public schools,” Creswell said. “And that’s so disturbing.”

She acknowledged that kids encounter AI on their phones and shared that her daughter said her friends like using ChatGPT better than Gemini. But she said that the fact that some kids use AI on personal devices doesn’t mean that schools should be encouraging adoption at young ages when kids’ brains are still developing, and risks remain largely unknown.

“There’s all kinds of dangerous things out in the world, but we don’t have our school district handing it to kids at their desk,” Creswell said.

Meanwhile, schools seem to be part of the transparency problem.

Creswell said that currently, the Chicago school district “is telling us their official line is that kids don’t have access to generative AI tools and that we don’t need a district-level policy on generative AI because they have guidebooks.” However, she’s reviewed Google’s CPS contracts, and they suggest that kids are interacting with generative AI and frontier models. The ongoing confusion has led parents to demand “a real policy” that the CPS “board votes on and that has the input of teachers, parents, students, and experts who are not being funded by the tech industry.”

Creswell told Ars that parents are particularly upset that Google is not transparent about how it processes and uses student data. She’s exhaustively researched Google contracts through Freedom of Information Act requests and told Ars that dozens of agreements she reviewed offer no indication of how CPS partners like Google—as well as Anthropic and OpenAI—are handling student data. Although she understands that “it’s probably just an honest statement of fact” that Google has built AI inextricably into Search, parents have a right to know how their children’s data is ingested into Google’s models, she said.

“Any sort of transparency on what is actually happening in terms of the data that they collect and what they do with it, that is absolutely missing,” Creswell said. And “it’s very unclear what data is being saved.”

To Creswell and other advocates, it seems likely that kids’ data is collected no matter what, since “even if you do ‘-AI,’ and you don’t get a text summary, your search data and what’s being returned is all basically inside the generative AI model.” Although Illinois law only allows commercial use of de-identified data, parents think Google should clearly explain what steps it takes to ensure data can’t be recombined to re-identify kids.

Ars reached out to Google to see if the company could clarify if Creswell’s read is accurate or if there’s any meaningful distinction between adding “-AI” to an individual query versus building a button that would automatically add “-AI” to every query. Google did not clarify, instead emphasizing that schools will control whether students can access the Gemini app, Gemini Notebook, and Search, with options to toggle each off individually, but no option to use Search without AI.

Currently, that policy specifies that “we receive customer data through the Core Services and process it according to the school’s (the customer’s) instructions. This customer data includes anything submitted, stored, sent, or received through Core Services by you or your school.” Google collects that data on that activity, as well as account information “like name, email address and password” and location information, the policy said.

The policy didn’t discuss Gemini but noted that data from using core services helps maintain services, improve personalization, and increase security. And “the data we collect in the Additional Services is used across our services to deliver, maintain, and improve our services; develop new services; provide personalized services; measure performance; communicate with you; and protect Google, our users, and the public.”

To parents, the policy provides no reassurance that their kids’ data isn’t part of Google’s plan to dominate the AI industry.

“We should not have any child data being collected in a school setting being used for commercial purposes,” Creswell said.

In Chicago, a recently re-elected CPS school board member, Karen Zaccor, is pushing for an AI moratorium in schools after talking with city and community colleges about an allegedly noticeable decline in reading, math, and writing skills from students graduating from CPS schools. In Emma’s case, Google allegedly suppressed chat logs, and the school withheld safety logs, frustrating parents who already viewed AI as more harmful than beneficial in education.

At the Google protest Wednesday, parents waved signs that threatened to eject Google from schools, with slogans like “No Google + No AI = Win/Win.” Many kids used favorite cartoon characters from shows like Beavis and Butthead and South Park to slam Google’s seemingly cynical approach to introducing AI in schools. One young girl wearing pink rain boots held up a cardboard sign, declaring in colorful markers, “I like to read!!”

At its peak, about 60 people stood outside Google’s downtown Chicago office, enduring the rain and listening to teachers and students who oppose reckless AI adoption.

One particularly moving speech came from a CPS student, Reese Mackey, who spoke for students. She challenged Google to more carefully weigh the long-term harms of aggressively pushing AI onto students by claiming they’ll be left behind and struggle to find jobs without AI skills. Creswell told Ars the opposite seems true, as Emma Doe’s lawsuit pointed to research branding Gemini as “the most dangerous marketed for use by youth” and “confirming the existence of AI-induced psychosis.”

“What about the future of our youth?” Mackey said. “What about the future of our minds?”

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#355. Ukraine says it can’t stop Russian strikes because Starshield is limited again

Report: Ukraine suspects speeds limits are intended to help Russia in peace talks.

Ukraine President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said today that Starshield has again been disabled on fast-moving drones in Ukrainian territory occupied by Russian forces, and that the US has provided “very little” information about the return of the restriction.

“We have detected speed restrictions on Starshield on our Ukrainian territory again, in the temporarily occupied parts of Ukraine,” Zelenskyy said in a speech at the 9th European Political Community Summit in Ireland. “The speed limit is back at 161 kilometers per hour—that’s 100 miles per hour.”

Zelenskyy’s allegation is that Starshield—the military variant of SpaceX’s Starlink satellite service—stops working on Ukrainian military drones when they travel above 161 kilometers per hour.

SpaceX today denied Zelenskyy’s allegation. “This is false. There has been no change,” SpaceX’s media relations department told Ars today when asked if SpaceX has any response to Zelenskyy’s statement that Starshield is being restricted again.

SpaceX has received multiple Pentagon contracts to give Ukraine access to the military-focused Starshield. SpaceX CEO Elon Musk said last month that any decisions on Starshield restrictions are made by the US government. The Pentagon inspector general’s office has since declined a request from US senators to investigate who ordered the restrictions.

Ukraine officials reportedly said today that they believe the limit was reinstated to put pressure on Ukraine in the latest round of peace talks with Russia. Zelenskyy said in today’s speech that without satellite connectivity on its drones, Ukraine cannot intercept Russian drones that fly in from the occupied parts of Ukraine.

It has often been unclear whether US and Ukrainian government officials discussing the restrictions were referring to Starlink, the civilian service that isn’t supposed to be used for offensive military purposes, or Starshield, which relies on the same technology but is intended for military use. But today, Zelenskyy clearly stated that the current restrictions affect Starshield.

“This directly affects our ability to intercept Russian jet-powered Shahed drones,” Zelenskyy said. “These drones come from those [occupied] areas, and because of the speed restrictions, we simply cannot catch up with them in time. Near the front line, we cannot use our aircraft. Only drone interceptors can operate there. And without the speed we need, shooting down these Shaheds becomes much harder. This means more Russian strikes get through, and more of our people are killed, our civilians are killed, by Russia.”

Ukraine officials reported the first round of restrictions about a month ago, but the limits were removed by October 24. Zelenskyy said today that Ukraine doesn’t know why the limit is in place again.

“We are trying to get the details from the American side, but so far we know very little,” Zelenskyy said. “But some things are very clear. If someone in America is making decisions that basically treat Ukrainian territory as if it is not Ukrainian, then this tells us where things are going. We need to be clear and never recognize Russia’s occupation of Ukrainian territory in any way.”

A Financial Times article today described Ukrainian officials’ allegations that the Starshield limit was put back in place to influence peace talks:

Senior Ukrainian officials told the FT they believed the speed restrictions were imposed in an effort to pressure Kyiv during the latest peace talks with Russia, which just concluded in Abu Dhabi. US special envoy Steve Witkoff and Donald Trump’s son-in-law, Jared Kushner, were in Moscow on Thursday to meet Russian President Vladimir Putin, after which they were set to travel to Ireland to meet Zelenskyy. One senior Ukrainian official said it appeared that Washington wanted to strengthen Russia’s position in the talks.

“These are not random errors, but systematic speed restrictions,” Zelenskyy said. “For us, this primarily means a significant complication in intercepting Russian jet-powered drones.”

Musk last month accused Ukraine of using the Starlink commercial service for offensive drone strikes, which he said violates the terms of service. A Ukraine official denied Musk’s accusation and said previous limits imposed by SpaceX already prevented Ukraine’s military from using the commercial Starlink service in the way Musk described.

As for the Starshield military variant, Musk has said that “Starshield systems are owned and controlled by the US government. Any limits on speed or usage are decided by the US military and ultimately POTUS.” Trump denied making the decision to limit Ukraine’s satellite connectivity, and Secretary of State Marco Rubio said the administration had “no foreknowledge” of the slowdown.

We contacted the White House today and will update this article if we get a response. European Commission spokesperson Thomas Regnier said that restrictions on Ukraine’s use of Starshield are unacceptable.

“It is not the first time that Ukraine is facing Starshield disruptions. These reports are deeply concerning and, if confirmed, unacceptable,” Regnier said at a press conference. “SpaceX must have a consistent approach in sharing its services with Ukraine. Starshield services must be restored urgently for Ukraine to keep its soldiers connected, to protect its citizens, and to defend its territory.”

#354. The haunting Masters meltdown that changed Rory McIlroy's career

Editor’s Note:  This story was originally published in April 2023.

Slumped on his club, head buried in his arm, Rory McIlroy looked on the verge of tears.

The then-21-year-old had just watched his ball sink into the waters of Rae’s Creek at Augusta National and with it, his dream of winning The Masters, a dream that had looked so tantalizingly close mere hours earlier.

As a four-time major winner and one of the most decorated names in the sport’s history, few players would turn down the chance to swap places with McIlroy heading into Augusta this week.

Yet on Sunday afternoon of April 10, 2011, not a golfer in the world would have wished to be in the Northern Irishman’s shoes.

A fresh-faced, mop-headed McIlroy had touched down in Georgia for the first major of the season with a reputation as the leading light of the next generation of stars.

An excellent 2010 had marked his best season since turning pro three years earlier, highlighted by a first PGA Tour win at the Quail Hollow Championship and a crucial contribution to Team Europe’s triumph at the Ryder Cup.

Yet despite a pair of impressive top-three finishes at the Open and PGA Championship respectively, a disappointing missed cut at The Masters – his first at a major – served as ominous foreshadowing.

McIlroy shot 74 and 77 to fall four strokes short of the cut line at seven-over par, a performance that concerned him enough to take a brief sabbatical from competition.

But one year on in 2011, any lingering Masters demons looked to have been exorcised as McIlroy flew round the Augusta fairways.

Having opened with a bogey-free seven-under 65 – the first time he had ever shot in the 60s at the major – McIlroy pulled ahead from Spanish first round co-leader Alvaro Quirós with a second round 69.

It sent him into the weekend holding a two-shot cushion over Australia’s Jason Day, with Tiger Woods a further stroke behind and back in the hunt for a 15th major after a surging second round 66.

And yet the 21-year-old leader looked perfectly at ease with having a target on his back. Even after a tentative start to the third round, McIlroy rallied with three birdies across the closing six holes to stretch his lead to four strokes heading into Sunday.

The youngster was out on his own ahead of a bunched chasing pack comprising Day, Ángel Cabrera, K.J. Choi and Charl Schwartzel. After 54 holes, McIlroy had shot just three bogeys.

“It’s a great position to be in … I’m finally feeling comfortable on this golf course,” McIlroy told reporters.

“I’m not getting ahead of myself, I know how leads can dwindle away very quickly. I have to go out there, not take anything for granted and go out and play as hard as I’ve played the last three days. If I can do that, hopefully things will go my way.

“We’ll see what happens tomorrow because four shots on this golf course isn’t that much.”

The truth can hurt, and McIlroy was about to prove his assessment of Augusta to be true in the most excruciating way imaginable.

His fourth bogey of the week arrived immediately. Having admitted to expecting some nerves at the first tee, McIlroy sparked a booming opening drive down the fairway, only to miss his putt from five feet.

Three consecutive pars steadied the ship, but Schwartzel had the wind in his sails. A blistering birdie, par, eagle start had seen him draw level at the summit after his third hole.

A subsequent bogey from the South African slowed his charge, as McIlroy clung onto a one-shot lead at the turn from Schwartzel, Cabrera, Choi, and a rampaging Woods, who shot five birdies and an eagle across the front nine to send Augusta into a frenzy.

Despite his dwindling advantage and the raucous Tiger-mania din ahead of him, McIlroy had responded well to another bogey at the 5th hole, draining a brilliant 20-foot putt at the 7th to restore his lead.

The fist pump that followed marked the high-water point of McIlroy’s round, as a sliding start accelerated into full-blown free-fall at the par-four 10th hole.

His tee shot went careening into a tree, ricocheting to settle between the white cabins that separate the main course from the adjacent par-three course. It offered viewers a glimpse at a part of Augusta rarely seen on broadcast, followed by pictures of McIlroy anxiously peering out from behind a tree to track his follow-up shot.

Though his initial escape was successful, yet another collision with a tree and a two-putt on the green saw a stunned McIlroy eventually tap in for a triple bogey. Having led the field one hole and seven shots earlier, he arrived at the 11th tee in seventh.

By the time his tee drive at the 13th plopped into the creek, all thoughts of who might be the recipient of the green jacket had long-since switched away from the anguished youngster. It had taken him seven putts to navigate the previous two greens, as a bogey and a double bogey dropped him to five-under – the score he had held after just 11 holes of the tournament.

Mercifully, the last five holes passed without major incident. A missed putt for birdie from five feet at the final hole summed up McIlroy’s day, though he was given a rousing reception as he left the green.

Mere minutes earlier, the same crowd had erupted as Schwartzel sunk his fourth consecutive birdie to seal his first major title. After starting the day four shots adrift of McIlroy, the South African finished 10 shots ahead of him, and two ahead of second-placed Australian duo Jason Day and Adam Scott.

McIlroy’s eight-over 80 marked the highest score of the round. Having headlined the leaderboard for most of the week, he finished tied-15th.

Tears would flow during a phone call with his parents the following morning, but at his press conference, McIlroy was upbeat.

“I’m very disappointed at the minute, and I’m sure I will be for the next few days, but I’ll get over it,” he said.

“I was leading this golf tournament with nine holes to go, and I just unraveled … It’s a Sunday at a major, what it can do.

“This is my first experience at it, and hopefully the next time I’m in this position I’ll be able to handle it a little better. I didn’t handle it particularly well today obviously, but it was a character-building day … I’ll come out stronger for it.”

Once again, McIlroy would be proven right.

Just eight weeks later in June, McIlroy rampaged to an eight-shot victory at the US Open. Records tumbled in his wake at Congressional, as he shot a tournament record 16-under 268 to become the youngest major winner since Tiger Woods at The Masters in 1997.

The historic victory kickstarted a golden era for McIlroy. After coasting to another eight-shot win at the PGA Championship in 2012, McIlroy became only the third golfer since 1934 to win three majors by the age of 25 with triumph at the 2014 Open Championship.

Before the year was out, he would add his fourth major title with another PGA Championship win.

And much of it was owed to that fateful afternoon at Augusta. In an interview with the BBC in 2015, McIlroy dubbed it “the most important day” of his career.

“If I had not had the whole unravelling, if I had just made a couple of bogeys coming down the stretch and lost by one, I would not have learned as much.

“Luckily, it did not take me long to get into a position like that again when I was leading a major and I was able to get over the line quite comfortably. It was a huge learning curve for me and I needed it, and thankfully I have been able to move on to bigger and better things.

“Looking back on what happened in 2011, it doesn’t seem as bad when you have four majors on your mantelpiece.”

McIlroy’s contentment came with a caveat: it would be “unthinkable” if he did not win The Masters in his career.

Yet as he prepares for his 15th appearance at Augusta National this week, a green jacket remains an elusive missing item from his wardrobe.

Despite seven top-10 finishes in his past 10 Masters outings, the trophy remains the only thing separating McIlroy from joining the ranks of golf immortals to have completed golf’s career grand slam of all four majors in the modern era: Gene Sarazen, Ben Hogan, Gary Player, Jack Nicklaus, and Tiger Woods.

A runner-up finish to Scottie Scheffler last year marked McIlroy’s best finish at Augusta, yet arguably 2011 remains the closest he has ever been to victory. A slow start in 2022 meant McIlroy had begun Sunday’s deciding round 10 shots adrift of the American, who teed off for his final hole with a five-shot lead despite McIlroy’s brilliant 64 finish.

At 33 years old, time is still on his side. Though 2022 extended his major drought to eight years, it featured arguably his best golf since that golden season in 2014.

And as McIlroy knows better than most, things can change quickly at Augusta National.

#351. Google really needs you to know it cares about smart glasses privacy

Public trust in smart glasses is very important for selling lots of smart glasses.

The world has changed in the ten years since Snap first launched its camera-equipped Spectacles. Not only has the smart glasses market grown, the number of people sharing videos online has also jumped significantly. Nowadays, thanks to the rise of platforms like TikTok and Reels, anyone can be an influencer. And the possibility of innocent passersby inadvertently being included in a viral clip is far higher than it was.

It is in this climate that Google launches its intelligent eyewear devices today. This year has already seen Meta catch heat for what people called its "pervert glasses" or "creep glasses." In addition to making people feel uncomfortable about being spied on or filmed without their consent, the controversy has also discouraged existing users of those glasses from continuing to wear them, not to mention possibly putting off potential buyers.

Let's start with what's happening on the glasses. It's basically table stakes at this point, but they all have an LED light on them to indicate when someone is taking a picture or recording a video. The light, which sits on the left side of the frame while the camera is next to the right eye, has sensors to detect if it's covered or tampered with and will prevent the capture of pictures or video.

"Unfortunately, some of the glasses that have launched in the past launched without any kind of detection for those things, and that broke trust." Payne said. "And that's a problem," he added. He also said Google's methods are "pretty effective," though that's something that we'll have to test for ourselves to verify.

And just as there are bad actors who will continue to try to break security protections for any safe in the world or online, there will be people attempting to get around Google's anti-tampering measures here. Payne said "there will always be ways around what we build, and then we'll try to fix those holes," and that he expects there will be an ongoing back-and-forth. "But nonetheless, we're very happy with where we're starting on this one."

One of the main functions of Google's smart glasses is the ability for Gemini AI to use the camera to see what the wearer is looking at and provide more relevant information. During this process, the company says that the camera visuals won't be stored in your Gemini history. When there are faces in view, Google says "your glasses are also designed to automatically redact bystanders' faces before Gemini processes the visuals."

The camera stream also won't be used in AI training or ad targeting, and Google says any data that is sent off the glasses for processing, say in the cloud or on your phone, is encrypted for transit. It's also promising to bring the Private AI Compute processing platform to glasses, enabling these new devices to use the "hardware-secured sealed cloud environment" for greater processing.

While the camera may be the most obvious recording sensor on these devices, they also have four microphones onboard to allow you to speak to Gemini or get translations for people around you. Google uses beamforming to help focus on the individuals you're looking at when deciding whose speech to translate, and the company also said it "designed Gemini to focus on your voice by default, and not other voices around you, using directional microphones on the glasses."

Payne said that the microphones will filter out "all the world audio, as we call it, the bystander audio," It helps prevent accidental recording of people in your vicinity, but also improves Gemini's effectiveness by making sure it doesn't confuse someone else's heated argument as your query.

Of course, to make the glasses more useful, you'll have to grant it access to your apps and personal information. The intelligent eyewear can work with apps like Messages, Keep or YouTube Music, for instance, to send replies or create lists for you. Like on most phones, you'll have to grant permission to specific apps before they can access the camera on your glasses, and even after you do, you can still add or revoke access for every app.

Google also said you can view your past interactions with Gemini, delete individual chats or all of them. You'll also be able to decide if your conversations are used to train AI. And to make sure there's no confusion over whether you left the glasses on or off, and if the microphones or camera might still be tuning in, all the new eyewear have a power switch with a clear red indicator to tell you when they've been shut down.

Most intriguing are the measures Google is implementing across its entire ecosystem. The company said it has protections in place "across Google apps and services to deter misuse of the glasses," and they're pretty far-reaching.

For one thing, Google Shopping and Google Ads will "ban the sale and advertising of stickers or accessories designed to hide recording lights."

The company is also working to cull the demand for videos of a harassing or creepy nature. Payne said that one of the problems Google has seen is a small number of people "who use glasses to take creepy videos."

YouTube will "prohibit content that depicts things like harassment and unwanted sexualization. Any video uploaded to YouTube — whether it's recorded on a phone, a camera, or glasses — has to follow these policies, and videos that don't are removed."

There are also considerations for apps that request permission to use the cameras on these devices. "There's multiple approval layers if an app wants to use the camera," Payne said. "And there's actually some use cases that we're just blanket cutting off."

To that end, Google said that the Play Store and API policies will "prohibit apps from using the camera for social live-streaming or real-time facial identification, and from changing the behavior of the outer indicator light." This live-streaming restriction also applies to YouTube.

Of course, since the eyewear will be compatible with iOS devices as well, it's possible that someone finds an app of that nature in the App Store and uses it. And though Google may ban the advertisement or sale of physical goods that can be used to hide the LED indicator, people could very easily procure one on Amazon or Temu. There's more that needs to be done against this problem, and Google alone cannot cover all bases.

Sure, there's the honor system approach that most modern recording devices already use. Things like requiring an 18+ Google account to activate an intelligent eyewear device and asking users to agree to not recording others without permission or in sensitive places are in place here as well.

But the responsibility of prevention of misuse doesn't just fall to the user or Google. There needs to be greater collaboration across the industry for these measures to be effective. Payne said that Google is sharing these statements now in an attempt to set an example and send a message. "We hope that it resonates with people, and we hope that as a byproduct of that, some others in this space say, 'You know what? We should probably tighten up some of our policies.'"

If it's able to get the attention and cooperation of other players in the space, Google stands to benefit. "We think that that will then earn a lot of trust back in the category, then we can focus a lot on how cool and valuable these are."

#350. Google rolls out Gemini 4 Hydrogen, finally replacing 3.8 Flash

Gemini 4 Hydrogen isn’t getting a slow, security-conscious release like Carbon and Argon.

Google is continuing its elemental AI naming scheme with the new Gemini 4 Hydrogen. These names are less descriptive than the Pro/Flash monikers of earlier releases, but Hydrogen’s purpose is clear: It replaces the faster, more economical Flash models most people in Google’s ecosystem use.

With Google’s recent Argon and Carbon releases, the rollout was staged and began with cybersecurity researchers. Even when general availability began, Google restricted access to users paying for its most expensive AI subscription. The limits for Hydrogen are much more relaxed, with access beginning immediately. This model has replaced Gemini 3.8 Flash across the company’s products.

Google claims that Hydrogen represents a major improvement versus the previous Flash model. It runs in one of four thinking levels (low, medium, high, and the new xhigh), and even in high mode, it’s 2.5 times faster than Flash 3.8. Plus, it reduces “fabricated claims” by more than 5x, according to Google’s analysis. Users will also see gains in code generation, with Hydrogen more than tripling 3.8 Flash’s score in Terminal-Bench. In some cases, Hydrogen even trades blows with Argon. It managed a score of 54 in the popular Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index with xhigh mode versus 50 for Argon.

Google believes Hydrogen will be a key part of how developers work with agentic coding tools. While it still positions Argon and Carbon as the best for long-horizon tasks and security, Hydrogen’s speed makes it ideal for generating simpler code and running tests without blowing through token budgets.

Hydrogen is also being integrated with Google Search, which the company says is the biggest update to its AI-powered search tools ever. The new model should better handle long, multipart questions, and the responses in both AI Overviews and AI Mode will have more visual elements. The company also claims Hydrogen’s fan-out search has been tuned to link to more sources, but we’ve heard that from Google before.

The U.S. secretary of state said that American economic pressure is doing sufficient damage to the leadership in Tehran.

Secretary of State Marco Rubio said on Tuesday that the Trump administration’s economic pressure campaign against Iran was effective enough that “there’s really no need for major combat operations any longer.”

Mr. Rubio’s declaration was the most explicit signal yet that President Trump does not intend to escalate his conflict with Iran after last week’s U.S. midterm elections.

Speaking to reporters in Paris, Mr. Rubio added that the United States might still respond with force if Iran “crosses certain red lines,” which he did not specify.

A day earlier, Mr. Trump told reporters that the war against Iran “militarily is effectively over.” But while Mr. Trump often opines off the cuff, Mr. Rubio lent an official veneer to that position by saying he had informed his counterparts about it on Tuesday at a meeting of Group of 7 foreign ministers.

Israeli officials and Iran hawks in Washington have called for more bombings in the war against Iran, which began more than eight months ago. But Mr. Rubio said that further American strikes were no longer necessary because a combination of U.S. economic sanctions and an American naval blockade “are doing devastation” to Iran’s economy.

He would not say how much longer Iran might be able to endure the American pressure, or what outcome the United States was pursuing.

#348. Jensen Huang is out as NVIDIA CEO

He has held the position since the company's founding.

On Thursday, NVIDIA's board of directors said that Huang had resigned his position as president and chief executive of the company, effective immediately. Huang also gave up his board seat. NVIDIA named Colette Kress, the company's current executive vice president and chief financial officer, as interim CEO.

"Kress will continue to serve as Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer while serving as Interim Chief Executive Officer until a permanent successor has been named," NVIDIA said. "The Board is initiating a search for a permanent Chief Executive Officer and will consider internal and external candidates."

As part of the shakeup, NVIDIA also appointed its first-ever chair of the board in Harvey Jones, who previously served as CEO and executive chairman of electronic design automation company Synopsys. "Colette has helped lead NVIDIA through a period of extraordinary growth and has a deep understanding of our business, customers, operations and financial position," Jones said in a statement. "She is supported by an experienced leadership team that has been instrumental in executing NVIDIA's strategy and advancing its technology roadmap. The Board has full confidence in Colette and the leadership team, and we expect the company's operations, customer commitments and product roadmap to continue without interruption."

Huang's departure comes days after NVIDIA's board said it would investigate Huang after he admitted to having an affair with a former tech executive accused of being a Chinese intelligence asset. The board said at the time it had "no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised." The FBI is also investigating, according to President Donald Trump.

The New York Times later reported that Huang had also had an affair with an NVIDIA executive who reported to him, and that the two were seen "being affectionate" at a hotel during a company conference this year. A bystander who reportedly recorded the interaction was offered a nondisclosure agreement by Huang's security detail, according to the report.

Huang's downfall is a stunning turn for the company, which has become a linchpin for the AI industry. He has been CEO since co-founding the company in 1993. Since then, he has transformed NVIDIA from a 3D graphics company to an AI powerhouse worth more than $4 trillion.

As NVIDIA's stock price has exploded in recent years Huang has become an increasingly public face of the AI boom. He often appeared at events — usually wearing his signature black leather jacket — with fellow tech CEOs like Mark Zuckerberg and Satya Nadella. At a time when high profile AI researchers are raising the alarm about superintelligence, Huang has had a much more positive view on the technology. He argued against increased regulation of the industry and has said America should not restrict China's access to US-made chips.

Huang has frequently been seen with President Trump, and was one of several tech leaders to accompany him on a recent trip to China. Asked about recent revelations, the president said he believed Huang was "an honorable guy" and that he "probably has an excuse for it."

In a memo to NVIDIA employees earlier this week in which he apologized for his actions, Huang wrote that leading the company was "the privilege of my life" and that he was "committed to protecting NVIDIA and supporting America's national security." He added that he was "fully committed to NVIDIA" and would "work to earn back your confidence." But the CEO who once seemed untouchable won't get the chance to do so.

“That’s the first time I really vividly heard the crowd during my race,” McIntosh said, per Reuters.

The Canadian teen made her Olympic debut aged 14 at Tokyo 2020, where she finished fourth in the 200-meter freestyle.

She bagged gold in the 200m butterfly and 400m medley at the world championships in 2022, as well as two golds at the Commonwealth Games in the 200m and 400m medleys.

She now trains in Florida under coach Brent Arckey with the Sarasota Sharks team.

#345. Pre-orders for some Apple Home Collection devices will open November 13

Apple hasn't revealed exactly when other products in the lineup — including thermostats and door locks — will be available, but the company has said some will be available next month with the rest debuting in early 2027.

The camera has IP65-rated protection from rain and dust and a two‑way talk function. As with the indoor camera, you'll get 10 days' worth of event video history via iCloud+. There's built-in storage too. The standard outdoor camera costs $300 and a version with a floodlight will run you $400.

As for the $250 video doorbell, that has a dual-camera design that can pick up (for instance) visitors and packages simultaneously — a 2.5K camera faces forward and a Full HD one points downward. You'll be able to view either feed separately in the Home app and chat to whoever's at your door using the two-way talk function. When someone rings the doorbell, HomeView and HomePod devices will sound a chime. You'll get notifications on other Apple devices too.

Again, there's low-light color night vision and IR night vision support, along with event video history in iCloud+ and built-in storage. The camera can detect people, pets, vehicles and packages and it has a dimmable spotlight.

#344. The Elder Scrolls' most obscure adventure gets a 4K PC makeover

N-Gage exclusive Shadowkey gets an AI-coded glow-up with plenty of new features.

So, you think you’re an Elder Scrolls superfan? You say you’ve been there since The Elder Scrolls Arena on MS-DOS and have logged hundreds of hours in The Elder Scrolls Online? You say you’ve bought all of the roughly 5,000 re-releases and remasters of Skyrim that have been made available in the last 15 years?

Tell me, then: What do you think of The Elder Scrolls Travels: Shadowkey?

What’s that? You’ve never played Shadowkey? You weren’t there day one back in 2004, Nokia N-Gage in hand, ready to experience the game’s 176 x 208 resolution mobile hack-and-slash RPG combat for yourself? And you call yourself a real Elder Scrolls fan?

Well, don’t worry. Now you can fill in this crucial gap in your history without even having to track down functional, decades-old N-Gage hardware. That’s thanks to Shadowkey: Reforged, a free fan-made PC port that pairs updated art with the original game files and scripts for an upscaled, 4K experience that runs natively on PC, no N-Gage or emulator needed.

Moving from a pre-iPhone mobile game to a modern PC obviously comes with major graphical upgrades, powered by a completely rewritten PC graphics engine. What was once a cramped portrait orientation on the N-Gage has now been expanded for a 16:9 monitor standard that displays at up to 120 fps (though the game’s logic still matches the original game’s 25 fps). In addition to newly retextured character and environmental models, the new port also sports small touches like bloom lighting, rippling water, and an upgraded interface with sharper text rendering than was possible on an ancient mobile phone.

Using the original game files (which you can obtain from an easy-to-find legitimate copy of the game, of course) means that Reforged‘s quests, dialogue, and game rules are “worked out from the original program,” as the game’s website puts it. That said, the game’s “Modern” mode adds a few touches like smarter enemy AI, fall damage, searchable containers, and a fast travel system that are missing from the original game (and from Reforged‘s more authentic “Classic” mode). The Modern mode also includes some welcome quality-of-life improvements like controller rumble, a smoother on-screen interface, and “better death poses.”

Those with a disposition against vibe-coded clones and ports should know that Reforged author Dan Whelan writes that he used “AI to assist with coding and building the engine, and with debugging,” as well as for the creation of “most of the modern, higher resolution artwork.” But those who are OK with this AI assistance should be happy to see the project moved from its first playable version in late October to a full-featured “release day” build for Windows, Mac, and Android that came out Wednesday. Whelan has been releasing daily updates to the project and seems set to continue, if the current “Version 0.2.9” designation is any indication.

The inherent limitations of 2000s-era N-Gage game development (which helped earn the original release some middling review scores) are still pretty apparent in even this Reforged remake. Still, it’s nice that Elder Scrolls completionists can now experience one of the series’ least-played releases. At the very least, it’s something to tinker with as we all continue the long wait for Elder Scrolls VI.

After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#343. US Soldier Is Arrested in UK on Suspicion of Spying for Russia

The 41-year-old man, who was not named, was in custody pending possible charges after a joint investigation with the F.B.I. and U.S. military intelligence.

Britain’s counterterrorism police said on Tuesday that they had arrested a member of the U.S. Army on suspicion of spying on behalf of Russia after a joint investigation with the F.B.I. and U.S. military intelligence.

The 41-year-old man was arrested near Gloucester, England, about 100 miles west of London, and was in custody pending possible charges, officials said.

Officials did not name the man but said in a statement that he had been detained on suspicion of violating Section 1 and Section 3 of Britain’s National Security Act 2023. Those sections relate to obtaining or disclosing protected information and assisting a foreign intelligence service. The police did not offer any other details, including about what the man was suspected of having done.

But Brig. Gen. Sean Stinchon, the commanding general of the U.S. Army Counterintelligence Command, said in the police’s statement, “The alleged conduct of a senior soldier, entrusted with sensitive information, is a sobering reminder that insider threats are real and persistent.”

In the police statement, the F.B.I.’s director, Kash Patel, is quoted as saying, “This important arrest involves allegations of espionage, which is of the utmost concern to the United States and our allies.”

Mr. Patel added, “While this investigation is continuing, I want to send a clear message — this F.B.I. has no tolerance for anyone who breaks their oath to the United States and whose actions endanger our national security and that of our partners.”

The Metropolitan Police also said in a statement, “The country to which the allegations relate is Russia.”

The Kremlin did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

The man arrested was based out of the Imjin Barracks at the headquarters of NATO’s Allied Rapid Reaction Corps in Gloucestershire, according to a U.S. military official who was briefed on the case and spoke on the condition of anonymity in order to discuss sensitive information. The base is NATO’s largest in Britain and hosts troops from 20 allied nations responsible for rapid response to crises in the alliance.

The counterterrorism police in Britain have investigated several high-profile terrorism cases related to the United States in the past months, including two cases of arrests near air bases in England where the American military operates.

In September, five men were arrested and then later released on bail near R.A.F. Fairford in southern England. Two Latvian men were arrested in October on suspicion of a national security offense after breaching the perimeter of R.A.F. Molesworth, about 70 miles north of London, in Cambridgeshire, England.

In the statement on Tuesday, the police said the arrest of the U.S. Army member on suspicion of espionage was “not linked to any other ongoing Counter Terrorism Policing investigations.”

Valerie Hopkins and Michael Schwirtz contributed reporting.

Michael D. Shear is the chief U.K. correspondent for The New York Times, covering British politics and culture and diplomacy around the world.

#339. Zelenskyy says Starshield has been slowed again, endangering Ukraine's defense

It comes three weeks after Ukraine’s speeds were restored following the last throttling incident.

As media headlines focus on Jensen Huang's resignation and the midterms fallout, you could almost forget that Ukraine is still fighting for its life — with all of Europe nervously watching. According to President Volodymyr Zelenskyy, his job just got harder again. The country's access to Starshield, the military version of Starlink, has been hit with "systematic speed restrictions" for several days. And it just so happens to be while Trump's envoys are in Moscow for talks with Russian President Vladimir Putin.

Zelenskyy posted on X that the speed limits are being imposed along the war's entire front line and in Russian-occupied territory. He said any terminal moving faster than 161 km/h (100 mph) loses its connection to the satellite network. Following briefings from his defense minister and top commander, Zelenskyy said Ukraine's military had investigated and ruled out random errors.

"Unfortunately, this is helping the Russians kill more Ukrainians and carry out more accurate strikes against Ukraine," Zelenskyy said. "We are trying to establish all the circumstances with the American side."

Ukraine's interceptor drones rely on Starshield to chase down Russia's jet-powered Shahed drones, which can fly up to 500 km/h (310 mph). Trying to catch them at 100 mph won't cut it. Zelenskyy says some strikes have already gotten through because Ukraine's interceptors couldn't go fast enough.

The timing is... curious. US envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner landed in Moscow today for talks with Putin. They're expected to meet Zelenskyy later at a summit in Ireland.

This isn't the first time Ukraine's Starshield access has been throttled. On October 14, Ukraine's military found that its speeds were being limited to around the same threshold. It happened as President Trump was publicly complaining about Ukraine's strikes on Russian refineries, which he blamed for high fuel prices in the US ahead of the midterms. Analysts agree that Trump's war with Iran has been the biggest factor in sky-high oil and diesel prices.

It still isn't clear who made the call to throttle Starshield the first time. Reuters, citing US officials, reported that the order came from inside the White House. But days later, the Wall Street Journal reported that Musk personally ordered the slowdown, possibly after talking with Trump. Trump denied any role, for whatever that's worth.

Musk's responses were all over the place. First, he said Starshield is "owned and controlled by the US government," then later claimed Ukraine had violated Starlink's terms of service. That's a strange thing to say about something you just said you don't own or control. Regardless, speeds were restored on October 24, reportedly after Trump called Musk and asked him to lift the caps amid bipartisan pressure from lawmakers.

Three weeks later, we're back where we started, with (so far) no one claiming responsibility.

#338. Ars Live: What drone attacks on data centers mean for modern life in the cloud

A livestream Ars discussion is happening on November 17 at 1 pm Eastern Time.

Drone and missile strikes have inflicted unprecedented damage on data centers in Eastern Europe and the Middle East this year, disrupting cloud services for millions of people and permanently deleting some Amazon Web Services customer data. This trend represents a new concern for Big Tech companies—but also for governments and everyone else who depends on common digital services in modern life.

To explore what this all means for modern society’s reliance on cloud resilience, I will be hosting a live conversation titled “Drone attacks and data centers: New Risks for Big Tech” on Tuesday, November 17 at 1 pm ET with Joseph Jarnecki, a senior research fellow at the Royal United Services Institute (RUSI), a UK defense and security think tank. He leads RUSI’s work on how cloud services factor into national security and defense, along with tech sovereignty efforts.

Jarnecki has done extensive research on how Ukraine strengthened its cloud resilience with Western partners despite facing both cyberattacks and kinetic attacks at the start of the full-scale Russian invasion, and explored what that means for NATO and European governments’ cloud adoption plans.

This year, Jarnecki has written about Iran’s drone and missile strikes on Amazon data centers in the Middle East during the US-Israeli war with Iran, and the broader implications for governments and Big Tech. He has also commented on the impacts of Russia and Ukraine exchanging strikes on each other’s data centers.

For more background on the data center attacks that have transpired so far in 2026, you can see my previous Ars coverage of how the Iranian strikes left Amazon with months of repairs, and caused enough damage to result in the permanent loss of some AWS customer data.

I’ve also covered why Russia was not ready for the data center war it started with Ukraine, with the systematic Russian strike campaign targeting Ukraine’s data centers provoking Ukrainian counterstrikes that damaged Russian government data centers and AI data centers owned by the Russian tech giant Yandex.

My conversation with Jarnecki will explore why data centers have become much more prominent military targets, whether defending or hardening data centers against drones and missiles is a realistic option, how cloud resiliency can be improved in wartime conditions, and the choices governments and cloud providers face in deploying data centers during an era of growing geopolitical risks.

You can watch the discussion live on YouTube on November 17 at 1 pm ET. We will also be fielding a few audience questions.

#333. ICE Deportation Flight Carrying Iranians Turns Back and Lands in Arizona

The Iranian immigrants in the plane were scheduled to be flown to Pakistan and then transferred to Iran, lawyers for the group said.

By Pranav BaskarHamed AleazizSalman Masood and Zach Montague

A U.S. deportation flight from Arizona carrying Iranians bound for Iran turned around midair and landed back in Arizona early Tuesday morning, according to a legal defense group and a flight-tracking monitor.

The Iranians on the flight were meant to be transferred to Iran from neighboring Pakistan, but the flight turned around after Pakistan abruptly declined to accept them, according to a relative of a detainee who said an Iranian official shared that information, and a U.S. official. Both of them spoke on the condition of anonymity to discuss confidential information.

By Tuesday afternoon, the Iranian immigrants were returned from the plane to the Arizona immigration detention facility, the relative said. He added that there were at least nine people in the group.

The plans have raised questions among immigration rights groups and lawyers about Pakistan’s role in facilitating deportations to countries where many immigrants fear persecution if they are sent back.

When asked about the deportations to Afghanistan via Pakistan at a news conference on Friday, Sajjad Haider Khan, a spokesman for Pakistan’s foreign ministry, said the arrangement was “under consideration.” He added that Pakistan is hopeful that a “mutually agreeable way forward will be reached.”

According to court filings by the Iranian American Legal Defense Fund, a group of Iranians who are detained at an Immigration and Customs Enforcement facility in Arizona were told by an Iranian government official over the weekend that they were going to be put on a flight to Pakistan with “onward transfer to Iran.” The legal defense fund has filed a lawsuit on behalf of Iranian detainees.

#332. In reversal, American Cancer Society urges routine prostate cancer screening

Men aged 50–69 and at average risk should get PSA screening every two years.

In a significant update to its guidelines for prostate cancer screening, the American Cancer Society is now recommending that men get routinely screened for prostate cancer with a PSA (prostate-specific antigen) test.

Men at average risk should get a PSA test every two years from age 50 to 69, according to the new guidelines released Thursday.

Men considered at high risk due to race and family history should start testing earlier, at 45, and most are recommended to get tested every year. Specifically, yearly testing starting at age 45 is recommended for all Black men, who have higher rates of prostate cancer. Non-Black men should also get yearly testing starting at 45 if a close relative was diagnosed with prostate cancer before age 65, or if more than one of their relatives has had prostate cancer. On the other hand, if only one close relative was diagnosed with prostate cancer and it was diagnosed at age 65 or older, non-Black men in this case should start PSA screening at 45, but are recommended to get testing only every two years.

Men ages 70 to 74 should consult their doctor on continuing screening, while those 75 and up are not recommended to get screened.

The new guidance departs from ACS’s previous iteration, published in 2010, which made no universal recommendations for routine screening. Men at age 50 were recommended to use shared-decision making with their doctors on whether or not to get tested. The recommendation was similar to that from the US Preventive Services Task Force, a federal panel that regularly evaluates evidence and makes recommendations on such preventive medical care. (The USPSTF is currently working on an update to its prostate cancer screening recommendation, though it should be noted that the panel has recently been overhauled by anti-vaccine Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. and new members are reportedly rife with conflicts of interest.)

In announcing the guidelines today, the ACS says its updates are based on new evidence since 2010 that shows screening saves lives, and that advances in case management can avoid the concerning harms from overdiagnosis and overtreatment. For many men with prostate cancer, their cancer will never grow or cause harm in their lifetime, while treatment can run the risk of erectile dysfunction and urinary incontinence.

In the new guidance, the ACS adds guardrails, firstly advising all men to be informed about the risks and benefits. If a PSA test comes back high—defined as 3 ng/mL or higher for men under 70 and 6.5 ng/mL or higher for those 70 and up—the ACS recommends repeating the test before taking any next steps.

If repeat testing is still high, then men should get either a prostate MRI or a validated biomarker test to assess risk. Only if there is a high suspicion of cancer—such as having a very high PSA test result—should men jump to a prostate biopsy.

For men diagnosed with prostate cancer, active surveillance should be the initial management strategy for men whose cancer appears to be at low risk of growing or spreading.

“By catching aggressive cancers with a simple PSA blood test, you open the door to highly effective treatments that protect your future,” Dr. Shanthi Sivendran, interim chief patient officer for the ACS, said in a statement. “Meanwhile, lower-risk findings can be safely managed through careful, active surveillance.”

Prostate cancer is one of the most common cancers in men and the second leading cause of cancer death in US men, the ACS notes. Nearly 334,00 men are estimated to be diagnosed with prostate cancer in 2026, and it’s estimated that over 36,320 will die from the disease.

This post was updated for clarity on November 12.

#331. Multiple Microsoft 365 services are experiencing an outage
